below, this process of adding new vertices does not alter the height coordinate function on the set of original vertices. This technique proves to be useful, and we will employ it in the proof of our main result (see, for instance, the setups for the proofs of Propositions
4.3
and
4.8
in Subsections
4.1
and
4.2
, respectively).

In order to make this precise, we start with the following definition.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
W\subset\mathbb{G}
be a finite subset of the metric graph. We define the weighted planar graph
(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},c^{\prime})
associated to
(\mathcal{G},c)
and
W
as follows:

1.

The set of vertices
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is given by
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}\cup W
;

2.

If the interior of an edge
e\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}
contains
n\in\mathbb{N}
points of
W
, then
e
is split into
n+1
new edges
\smash{[e^{\prime}_{n+1}]}
according to the points in the interior of
e
. The edge
e
remains unchanged otherwise.

3.

If the interior of an edge
e\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}
is split into
\smash{[e^{\prime}_{n+1}]}
new edges, for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then we set

c^{\prime}_{e^{\prime}_{i}}:=\frac{c_{e}}{\mathrm{d}^{\mathbb{G}}\bigl(e_{i}^{\prime,-},e_{i}^{\prime,+}\bigr)},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall i\in[n+1].

The conductance of
e
remain unchanged otherwise.

The weighted dual graph
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},\widehat{c}^{\prime})
can be naturally constructed from
(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},c^{\prime})
.

Remark 3.2
.

At the level of the Smith diagram, adding new vertices to the interior of some edges of the graph according to the procedure described above corresponds to horizontally dissecting the rectangles associated to such edges. More precisely, let us assume for simplicity that only one point is added to the interior of an edge
e
, and let
e_{1}^{\prime}
and
e_{2}^{\prime}
be the new edges in which
e
is split into. Suppose that
\smash{e_{1}^{\prime,-}=e^{-}}
and
\smash{e_{2}^{\prime,+}=e^{+}}
. Let
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
be the tiling map associated to new weighted graph. Then it is immediate to check that
\mathcal{S}(e)=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(e_{1}^{\prime})\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(e_{2}^{\prime})
. In particular the rectangles
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(e_{1}^{\prime})
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(e_{2}^{\prime})
have the same width of the rectangle
\mathcal{S}(e)
, and the height of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(e_{1}^{\prime})
is proportional to
\smash{\mathrm{d}^{\mathbb{G}}(e_{1}^{\prime,-},e_{1}^{\prime,+})}
, while that of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(e_{2}^{\prime})
is proportional to
\smash{\mathrm{d}^{\mathbb{G}}(e_{2}^{\prime,-},e_{2}^{\prime,+})}
. We refer to Figure
5
for a diagrammatic representation of this procedure.

Figure 5:
An edge
e
is split into two edges,
\smash{e_{1}^{\prime}}
and
\smash{e_{2}^{\prime}}
, with conductances
c_{e^{\prime}_{1}}
and
c_{e^{\prime}_{2}}
, as specified in Definition
3.1
, by adding a new blue vertex. On the right, the corresponding original rectangle
\smash{\mathcal{S}(e)}
is split into two rectangles
\smash{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(e_{1}^{\prime})}
and
\smash{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(e_{2}^{\prime})}
such that
\smash{\mathcal{S}(e)=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(e_{1}^{\prime})\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(e_{2}^{\prime})}
. Similarly, a dual edge is split into two dual edges with suitable conductances by adding a new green dual vertex. On the right, the corresponding original rectangle is then split into the union of two rectangles.

Let
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}:\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to[0,1]
be the voltage function associated to the quadruple
(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},c^{\prime},v_{0},v_{1})
. The following lemma relates the function
\mathfrak{h}
with
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 3.3
.

For every
x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}
, it holds that
\mathfrak{h}(x)=\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}(x)
.

Proof.

We denote by
K:=\#\{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}\}
the number of new vertices added to the graph
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. If
K=0
, then the result follows immediately. Let us now assume that
K=1
and suppose that the edge
e\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}
is split into two new edges
e_{1}^{\prime}
and
e_{2}^{\prime}
with conductances
\smash{c^{\prime}_{e_{1}^{\prime}}}
and
\smash{c^{\prime}_{e_{2}^{\prime}}}
as specified in Definition
3.1
. Then, the desired result follows immediately from the series law of electrical network (cf.
[
LP16
, Section 2.3.I]
). The general case follows from a simple induction argument on
K
.
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

For
x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}
, let
X^{\prime,x}
be the random walk on
(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},c^{\prime})
started from
x
. Let
\uptau_{0}:=0
and, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we define inductively
\uptau_{k+1}:=\inf\{j>\uptau_{k}\,:\,X^{\prime,x}_{j}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}\}
. Then
\{X_{\uptau_{k}}^{\prime,x}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
has the same distribution as the random walk on
(\mathcal{G},c)
started from
x
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
3.3
, let
K:=\#\{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}\}
. If
K=0
, then the result is obvious. Let us now assume that
K=1
and let
y\in W
be the vertex added to
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. We claim that the distribution of
X^{\prime,x}_{\uptau_{1}}
is equal to that of
X^{x}_{1}
. If all the edges in
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}(x)
do not contain
y
, then the claim is obvious. Let us assume that there exists an edge in
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}(x)
which contains
y
. Then, thanks to an easy computation, one can verify that

\mathbb{P}_{x}(X^{\prime}_{\uptau_{1}}=v)=\frac{c_{xv}}{\pi(x)},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall v\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}(x).

Therefore, thanks to the strong Markov property of
X^{\prime,x}
, we get that for all
k\geq 2
, it holds that

\mathbb{P}_{x}(X^{\prime}_{\uptau_{k}}=v\mid X^{\prime}_{\uptau_{k-1}}=w)=\begin{cases}c_{vw}/\pi(w),&v\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}(w)\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}\hskip 10.00002pt\forall k\geq 2,

which proves the result if
K=1
. The general case follows from a simple induction argument on
K
.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

Given a finite set
\widehat{W}\subset\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
, following a similar procedure to the one described above, one can also construct the weighted dual graph
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},\widehat{c}^{\prime})
associated to
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}},c)
and
\widehat{W}
. In particular, results similar to the one stated above hold also for the respective dual counterparts. For example, if
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}:\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}/\upeta\mathbb{Z}
is the width coordinate function associated to the new weighted graph, then
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
restricted to
\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}
coincides with the original width coordinate function. Moreover, adding new dual vertices to the interior of some dual edges corresponds to vertically dissecting the associated rectangles in the Smith diagram. We refer to Figure
5
for a diagrammatic representation of this procedure.

3.2
Periodicity

We collect here some properties of the lifted Smith diagram that are due to its periodicity. We recall that the map
\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}
is defined in Definition
2.15
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\mathbf{x}_{1}
,
\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger}}
be such that
\upsigma_{2\pi}(\mathbf{x}_{1})=\upsigma_{2\pi}(\mathbf{x}_{2})
. Then, it holds almost surely that

\left|\frac{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}(\mathbf{x}_{2}))-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}(\mathbf{x}_{1}))}{\upeta}-\frac{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{x}_{2})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{x}_{1})}{2\pi}\right|\leq 1.

Proof.

We observe that the proof of this result is an easy consequence of Lemma
2.9
. However, it does not follow immediately from Lemma
2.9
since the points
\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}(\mathbf{x}_{1})
and
\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}(\mathbf{x}_{2})
are points chosen uniformly at random from the horizontal segments
\mathcal{S}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{x}_{1})
and
\mathcal{S}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{x}_{2})
, respectively. We give here the details for completeness.

Let
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},\uparrow}(\mathbf{x}_{1})=[\mathbf{e}^{1}_{n}]
be the set of lifted harmonically oriented edges with tails equals to
\mathbf{x}_{1}
ordered in such a way that
\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{1}_{1}\cdots\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{1}_{n}
forms a path in
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger}}
oriented from left to right. Define
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},\uparrow}(\mathbf{x}_{2})=[\mathbf{e}^{2}_{n}]
in a similar way. Then, by construction of the map
\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}
, it holds that

\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}(\mathbf{x}_{1}))\in\bigl[\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{1,-}_{1}),\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{1,+}_{n})\bigr],\hskip 20.00003pt\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}(\mathbf{x}_{2}))\in\bigl[\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{2,-}_{1}),\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{2,+}_{n})\bigr].

Moreover, by Lemma
2.9
, we have that

\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{1,-}_{1})-\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{2,-}_{1})=\frac{\upeta}{2\pi}\left(\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{x}_{1})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{x}_{2})\right),\hskip 20.00003pt\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{1,+}_{n})-\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{2,+}_{n})=\frac{\upeta}{2\pi}\left(\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{x}_{1})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{x}_{2})\right),

and also

\bigl|\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{1,+}_{n})-\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{1,-}_{1})\bigr|=\bigl|\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{2,+}_{n})-\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{2,-}_{1})\bigr|\leq\upeta,

where the first equality is again due to Lemma
2.9
, and the last inequality is due to the fact that, by construction of the Smith diagram, every horizontal line segment has width at most
\upeta
.
Therefore, putting together all these facts, one can readily obtain the desired result.
∎

In order to state and prove the next lemma of this subsection, we need to introduce some notation. Let
a\in(0,1)
, and assume that all points in the set
\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)
are vertices in
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}
. We define the set of harmonically oriented edges
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}_{a}
and the corresponding set of dual edges
\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{a}
as follows

\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}_{a}:=\bigl\{e\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}\,:\,\mathfrak{h}(e^{-})=a<\mathfrak{h}(e^{+})\bigr\},\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{a}:=\bigl\{\widehat{e}\in\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}\,:\,\widehat{e}\text{ is the dual of some }e\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}_{a}\bigr\},

and we define the following sets of vertices

\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}_{a}:=\bigl\{x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}\,:\,x=e^{-}\text{ for some }e\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}_{a}\bigr\},\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{a}:=\bigl\{\widehat{x}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}\,:\,\widehat{x}=\widehat{e}^{-}\text{ for some }\widehat{e}\in\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{a}\bigr\},

i.e.,
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}_{a}
is nothing but
\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)
.
Furthermore, we denote by
\smash{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}_{a}^{{\dagger}}}
,
\smash{\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger}}_{a}}
,
\smash{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}_{a}^{{\dagger}}}
,
\smash{\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{a}^{{\dagger}}}
the lifts to the universal cover of the corresponding objects.

Now, we let
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}^{a}_{0}}
be the vertex in
\smash{\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger}}_{a}}
whose real coordinate is nearest to
0
. We note that the set
\smash{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}_{a}}
is a cutset and that the associated set of oriented dual edges
\smash{\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{a}}
admits an enumeration
\smash{[\widehat{e}_{n}]}
such that
\smash{\widehat{e}_{1}\cdots\widehat{e}_{n}}
forms a counter-clockwise oriented noncontractible simple closed loop in the dual graph
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{G}}}
. In particular, every edge
\smash{\widehat{e}_{i}}
admits a lift
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}}
such that
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{1}\cdots\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{n}}
forms a simple path oriented from left to right joining
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}^{a}_{0}}
to the shifted vertex
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}^{a}_{0}+(0,2\pi)}
(see Lemma
2.2
). We refer to Figure
6
for a diagrammatic representation.

Definition 3.7
.

Letting
[\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{n}]
be as specified above, we define the set of lifted dual vertices

\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{a}^{\dagger}:=\bigl\{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger}}_{a}\,:\,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}=\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{-}\text{ for some }i\in[n]\bigr\},

(3.1)

and we also define the set of lifted vertices

\mathcal{P}_{a}^{\dagger}:=\bigl\{\mathbf{x}\in(\mathfrak{h}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(a)\,:\,\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{e}_{i}^{-}\text{ for some }i\in[n]\bigr\},

(3.2)

where
[\mathbf{e}_{n}]
is the set of lifted edges associated to
[\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{n}]
.

Remark 3.8
.

Consider the infinite strip
\smash{\mathrm{S}^{a}_{2\pi}:=\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{a}),\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{a})+2\pi\bigr)\times\mathbb{R}}
. We remark that in general the set
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{a}^{\dagger}}
is not fully contained in
\smash{\mathrm{S}^{a}_{2\pi}}
.
We also observe that, thanks to the fact that
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}_{a}
is a cutset, it holds that
\smash{\upsigma_{2\pi}(\mathcal{P}_{a}^{\dagger})=\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)}
.

We are now ready to state and prove the next lemma of this subsection. We refer to Figure
6
for a diagram illustrating the various objects involved in the proof of Lemma
3.9
.

Figure 6:
A diagram illustrating the proof of Lemma
3.9
.
Left:
A portion of the lifted graph
\smash{\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger}}}
embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The blue/green vertices are the vertices in the set
\smash{\mathcal{P}_{a}^{\dagger}}
. The green vertex
\mathbf{x}
is a fixed vertex in
\smash{\mathcal{P}_{a}^{\dagger}}
. The red dual vertices are the vertices in
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{a}^{\dagger}}
. The blue edges belong to the set
\smash{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}_{a}^{{\dagger}}}
, and the red dual edges belong to the set
\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger}}_{a}
. The leftmost red dual vertex corresponds to the vertex
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}^{a}_{0}
. The two red dual edges with an arrow are the edges
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{1}^{\mathbf{x}}}
and
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\mathbf{x}}}
.
Right:
A portion of the tiling of
\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
constructed via the lifted tiling map
\mathcal{S}^{{\dagger}}
. The blue horizontal line corresponds to
\smash{\cup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{a}^{\dagger}}\mathcal{S}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{x})}
. The vertical red segments correspond to
\smash{\mathcal{S}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{1}^{\mathbf{x},-})}
and
\smash{\mathcal{S}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\mathbf{x},+})}
. The green point corresponds to a possible realization of
\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}(\mathbf{x})
.

Lemma 3.9
.

Fix
a\in(0,1)
and assume that all the points in
\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)
are vertices in
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}
.
Let
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}^{a}_{0}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{a}^{{\dagger}}
be as specified above, then it holds that

0\leq\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})-\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}}^{a}_{0})\leq\upeta,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{a}^{\dagger},

and also, it holds almost surely that

0\leq\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}(\mathbf{x}))-\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}}^{a}_{0})\leq\upeta,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{a}^{\dagger}.

Proof.

We start by proving the first part of the lemma. We consider the set of dual edges
[\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{n}]
such that
\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{1}\cdots\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{n}
forms a simple path oriented from left to right joining
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}^{a}_{0}
to the shifted vertex
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}^{a}_{0}+(0,2\pi)
, as specified before the lemma statement. Thanks to Lemma
2.9
, we know that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\nabla\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{i})=\upeta,

and each summand in the sum is non-negative. Now, let
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{a}^{\dagger}
, then there exists a subpath
\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}_{1}\cdots\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}_{k}
of
\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{1}\cdots\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{n}
which connects
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}^{a}_{0}
to
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}
. Therefore, we have that

\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})-\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}}^{a}_{0})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\nabla\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}_{i}),

and the conclusion then follows thanks to the fact that

0\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\nabla\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}_{i})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\nabla\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{i})=\upeta.

We now prove the second part of the lemma. To this end, we fix
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{a}^{\dagger}
, and we let
\smash{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},\uparrow}(\mathbf{x})=[\mathbf{e}^{\mathbf{x}}_{k}]}
be the set of lifted harmonically oriented edges with tails equal to
\mathbf{x}
ordered in such a way that the corresponding lifted dual edges
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\cdots\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{\mathbf{x}}_{k}}
forms a path in
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger}}}
oriented from left to right (see Figure
6
). By construction of the map
\smash{\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}}
, it holds that

\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}(\mathbf{x}))\in\bigl[\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{\mathbf{x},-}_{1}),\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{\mathbf{x},+}_{k})\bigr].

Finally, since both
\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{\mathbf{x},-}_{1}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{\mathbf{x},+}_{k}
belong to
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{a}^{\dagger}
, the conclusion follows thanks to the first part of the lemma.
∎

3.3
Hitting distribution of a horizontal line

Roughly speaking, the main goal of this subsection is to characterize the hitting distribution of a horizontal line on the Smith diagram for the Smith embedded random walk. Before precisely stating the result, we need to introduce some notation.

Definition 3.10
.

We define the map
\length:\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}\to[0,\upeta)
as the function that assigns to each vertex
x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}
the length of the horizontal segment
\mathcal{S}(x)
associated to
x
by the Smith embedding, i.e., we let

\length(x):=\sum_{\mathbf{e}\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{\downarrow}(x)}\nabla\mathfrak{h}(e),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G},

where
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{\downarrow}(x)
denotes the set of harmonically oriented edges in
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}
with heads equal to
x
.

We recall that the difference of the width coordinate function between the endpoints of a dual edge is equal to the gradient of the height coordinate of the corresponding primal edge. Moreover, thanks to the fact that the height coordinate function is harmonic, it is plain to see that

\length(x)=\sum_{\mathbf{e}\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{\uparrow}(x)}\nabla\mathfrak{h}(e),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G},

where
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{\uparrow}(x)
denotes the set of harmonically oriented edges in
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}
with tails equal to
x
. We can also naturally extend the definition of the length function to the metric graph
\mathbb{G}
. More precisely, given a point
x\in\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}
, if
x
lies in the interior of the edge
e\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}
, then we set
\length(x):=\nabla\mathfrak{h}(e)
.

Definition 3.11
.

Given a value
a\in(0,1)
, we define on the set
\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)\subset\mathbb{G}
the measure
\mu_{a}
by letting

\mu_{a}(x):=\frac{\length(x)}{\upeta},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall x\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a).

Since, by construction of the Smith diagram, it holds that
\smash{\sum_{x\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)}\length(x)=\upeta}
, the measure
\mu_{a}
is a probability measure on the set
\smash{\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)}
.

Remark 3.12
.

We emphasize that, thanks to Remark
3.8
, one can also view
\mu_{a}
as a probability measure on the set
\mathcal{P}_{a}^{\dagger}
, where we recall that
\mathcal{P}_{a}^{\dagger}
is the set defined in (
3.2
).

From now on, we assume throughout the whole subsection that all the points in the set
\cup_{x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}}\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(\mathfrak{h}(x))
are vertices in
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}
. At the level of the Smith diagram, this means that for all
x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}
, it holds that the set
\smash{\cup_{y\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(\mathfrak{h}(x))}\mathcal{S}(y)}
is equal to the noncontractible closed loop
\smash{\mathbb{R}/\upeta\mathbb{Z}\times\{\mathfrak{h}(x)\}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\upeta}}
. We observe that, for any given finite weighted planar graph, we can always canonically construct from it another finite weighted planar graph that satisfies this assumption. Indeed, suppose that there is a vertex
x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}
such that
\smash{\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(\mathfrak{h}(x))\not\subset\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}}
, then, using the procedure described in Subsection
3.1
, we can declare all the points in the finite set
\smash{\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(\mathfrak{h}(x))\setminus\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}}
to be vertices of the graph.

We fix a value
a\in(0,1)
such that all the points in the set
\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)
are vertices in
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}
, and we let
X^{\mu_{a}}
be the random walk on
(\mathcal{G},c)
started from a point in
\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)
sampled according to the probability measure
\mu_{a}
.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
X^{\mu_{a}}
be as specified above. For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that a finite sequence of height coordinates
[a_{N}]_{0}\subset(0,1)
is
admissible
for the random walk
X^{\mu_{a}}
if
a_{0}=a
and

\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(\mathfrak{h}(X_{n+1})=a_{n+1}\mid\mathfrak{h}(X_{n})=a_{n}\bigr)>0,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall n\in[N-1]_{0}.

We can now state the main result of this subsection.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
X^{\mu_{a}}
be as specified above. For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
[a_{N}]_{0}\subset(0,1)
be an admissible sequence of height coordinates for the random walk
X^{\mu_{a}}
. Then, for all
i\in[N]_{0}
, it holds that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(X_{i}=x_{i}\mid\{\mathfrak{h}(X_{n})=a_{n}\}_{n=1}^{N}\bigr)=\mu_{a_{i}}(x_{i}),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall x_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a_{i}).

Intuitively, the proof of the above lemma goes as follows. If
i=1
and we only had the conditioning on the event
\mathfrak{h}(X_{1})=a_{1}
, then the result would follow from a simple computation. However, since, in general,
i\neq 1
, and since we are also conditioning on the events
\{\mathfrak{h}(X_{n})=a_{n}\}_{n=1}^{i-1}
and
\{\mathfrak{h}(X_{n})=a_{n}\}_{n=i+1}^{N}
, which represent the height coordinates of the random walk for past and future times respectively, the proof of the result is not immediate. However, the proof follows by a simple induction argument in which we show that we can “forget” about the conditioning on past and future times. Roughly speaking, the reason why we can forget about past times is due to the fact that the height component of the random walk is itself a Markov process, while the reason why we can forget about future times is due to the harmonicity of the height coordinate function. We now proceed with the proof of the lemma.

Proof of Lemma
3.14
.

The proof involves three main steps.

Step 1:
We start by proving that
\smash{\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}(\mathfrak{h}(X_{1})=a_{1}\mid X_{0}=x_{0})=\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}(\mathfrak{h}(X_{1})=a_{1})}
, for all
\smash{x_{0}\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)}
. Since we are assuming that
\smash{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}=\cup_{x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}}\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(\mathfrak{h}(x))}
, at its first step, the random walk can only reach two heights: height
a_{1}
or height
\widetilde{a}_{1}
say. If
a_{1}>a
, since
\mathfrak{h}
is harmonic at
x_{0}
, it holds that

\mathfrak{h}(x_{0})=\frac{\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{\downarrow}(x_{0})}c_{e}\mathfrak{h}(e^{-})+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{\uparrow}(x_{0})}c_{e}\mathfrak{h}(e^{+})}{\pi(x_{0})}=\widetilde{a}_{1}\frac{\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{\downarrow}(x_{0})}c_{e}}{\pi(x_{0})}+a_{1}\frac{\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{\uparrow}(x_{0})}c_{e}}{\pi(x_{0})},

(3.3)

where, as usual,
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{\downarrow}(x_{0})
(resp.
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{\uparrow}(x_{0})
) denotes the set of harmonically oriented edges with heads (resp. tails) equal to
x_{0}
.
Now, we observe that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(\mathfrak{h}(X_{1})=a_{1}\mid X_{0}=x_{0}\bigr)=\frac{\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{\uparrow}(x_{0})}c_{e}}{\pi(x_{0})},\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(\mathfrak{h}(X_{1})=\widetilde{a}_{1}\mid X_{0}=x_{0}\bigr)=\frac{\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{\downarrow}(x_{0})}c_{e}}{\pi(x_{0})}.

In particular, plugging these expressions into (
3.3
) and rearranging, we obtain that

\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathfrak{h}(X_{1})=a_{1}\mid X_{0}=x_{0}\bigr)=\frac{|\widetilde{a}_{1}-a|}{|\widetilde{a}_{1}-a_{1}|},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall x_{0}\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a),

from which the desired result follows since the right-hand side of the above expression does not depend on the particular choice of
\smash{x_{0}\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)}
. A similar conclusion also holds if
a_{1}<a
. Now, proceeding by induction, one can prove that for any
i\in[N]_{0}
and for all
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a),\ldots,x_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a_{i})
, it holds that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(\{\mathfrak{h}(X_{i+n})=a_{i+n}\}_{n=1}^{N-i}\mid\{X_{j}=x_{j}\}_{j=0}^{i}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}(\{\mathfrak{h}(X_{i+n})=a_{i+n}\}_{n=1}^{N-i}\mid\mathfrak{h}(X_{i})=a_{i}).

Step 2:

Thanks to the previous step and Bayes’ rule, we have that
\smash{\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(X_{0}=x_{0}\mid\mathfrak{h}(X_{1})=a_{1}\bigr)=\mu_{a}(x_{0})}
, for all
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)
. In particular, using this fact, we will now prove that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(X_{1}=x_{1}\mid\mathfrak{h}(X_{1})=a_{1}\bigr)=\mu_{a_{1}}(x_{1}),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall x_{1}\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a_{1}).

To this end, fix
x_{1}\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a_{1})
and suppose that
a_{1}>a
. Then we can proceed as follows

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(X_{1}=x_{1}\mid\mathfrak{h}(X_{1})=a_{1}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\sum_{x_{0}\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)\cap\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}(x_{1})}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\bigl(X_{1}=x_{1}\mid\mathfrak{h}(X_{1})=a_{1}\bigr)\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(X_{0}=x_{0}\mid\mathfrak{h}(X_{1})=a_{1}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\sum_{x_{0}\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)\cap\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}(x_{1})}\frac{c_{x_{0}x_{1}}}{\sum_{v\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a_{1})\cap\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}(x_{0})}c_{x_{0}v}}\mu_{a}(x_{0})

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\sum_{x_{0}\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)\cap\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}(x_{1})}\frac{c_{x_{0}x_{1}}}{\sum_{v\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a_{1})\cap\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}(x_{0})}c_{x_{0}v}}\frac{|a_{1}-a|\sum_{v\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a_{1})\cap\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}(x_{0})}c_{x_{0}v}}{\upeta}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\frac{1}{\upeta}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{\downarrow}(x)}\nabla\mathfrak{h}(e)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\mu_{a_{1}}(x),

where
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{\downarrow}(x)
denotes the set of harmonically oriented edges with heads equal to
x
. In order to pass from the second line to the third line of the above display, we used the definition of the probability measure
\mu_{a}
, and the fact that, for all
e\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{\downarrow}(x)
, it holds that
\mathfrak{h}(e^{-})=a
and
\mathfrak{h}(e^{+})=a_{1}
. One can proceed similarly if
a_{1}<a
. Now, proceeding again by induction, one can easily prove that, for all
i\in[N]
, it holds that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(X_{i}=x_{i}\mid\{\mathfrak{h}(X_{n})=a_{n}\}_{n=1}^{i}\bigr)=\mu_{a_{i}}(x_{i}),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall x_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a_{i}).

Step 3:
For
i\in[N]_{0}
, we observe that a consequence of Step 1 is that the sequence of random variables
\{X_{j}\}_{j=0}^{i}
is conditionally independent from
\{\mathfrak{h}(X_{i+n})\}_{n=1}^{N-i}
given
\mathfrak{h}(X_{i})=a_{i}
. Therefore, the conditional law of
\{X_{j}\}_{j=0}^{i}
given
\{\mathfrak{h}(X_{n})=a_{n}\}_{n=1}^{i}
is the same as the conditional law given
\{\mathfrak{h}(X_{n})=a_{n}\}_{n=1}^{N}
. Hence, this implies that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(X_{i}=x_{i}\mid\bigl\{\mathfrak{h}(X_{n})=a_{n}\bigr\}_{n=1}^{N}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(X_{i}=x_{i}\mid\bigl\{\mathfrak{h}(X_{n})=a_{n}\bigr\}_{n=1}^{i}\bigr),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall x_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a_{i}),

and so the conclusion follows from Step 2.
∎

3.4
Expected horizontal winding

Roughly speaking, the main goal of this subsection is to establish that the average winding that the Smith-embedded random walk does before hitting a given horizontal line on the Smith diagram is zero. Before precisely stating the result, we need to introduce some notation.

Definition 3.15
.

Given the random walk
\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{x}}
on the lifted weighted graph
(\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger}},c^{{\dagger}})
started from
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger}}
, we let

\mathbb{N}_{0}\ni n\mapsto\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathbf{x}}_{n}

be the discrete time process taking values in
\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
such that, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the conditional law of
\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathbf{x}}_{n}
, given
\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{x}}_{n}
, is equal to the law of
\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}(\mathbf{X}_{n}^{\mathbf{x}})
, where we recall that
\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}
is defined in Definition
2.15
. We call the process
\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathbf{x}}
the
lifted Smith-embedded random walk
associated to
\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{x}}
.

It follows from the definition of
\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathbf{x}}
that, at each time step
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the location of the point
\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathbf{x}}_{n}
is sampled uniformly at random and independently of everything else from the horizontal line segment
\smash{\mathcal{S}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{x}}_{n})}
.
With a slight abuse of notation, we also denote by
\smash{\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathbf{x}}}
the continuous time version
\smash{\{\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathbf{x}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}}
, where the continuous path is generated by piecewise linear interpolation at constant speed.

We assume also in this subsection that all the points in the set
\cup_{x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}}\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(\mathfrak{h}(x))
are vertices in
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}
. Furthermore, we fix a value
a\in(0,1)
such that all the points in the set
\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)
are vertices in
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}
, and we let
X^{\mu_{a}}
be the random walk on
(\mathcal{G},c)
started from a point in
\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)
sampled according to the probability measure
\mu_{a}
defined in Definition
3.11
. We also adopt the convention to denote by

\mathbb{N}_{0}\ni n\mapsto\mathbf{X}^{\mu_{a}}_{n}

the lift of the random walk
X^{\mu_{a}}
to the lifted weighted graph
(\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger}},c^{{\dagger}})
started from a point in
\mathcal{P}_{a}^{\dagger}
sampled according to the probability measure
\mu_{a}
(see Remark
3.12
). Moreover, we denote by
\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{\mu_{a}}
the lifted Smith-embedded random walk associated to
\mathbf{X}^{\mu_{a}}
as specified above.

In complete analogy with the definition of winding of a path in the infinite cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{2\pi}
, we have the following definition of winding on the cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{\upeta}
.

Definition 3.16
.

Let
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}
, consider a path
P:[t_{1},t_{2}]\to\mathcal{C}_{\upeta}
, and let
\mathbf{P}:[t_{1},t_{2}]\to\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
be its lift to the covering space
\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
. Then, we define the winding of
P
as follows

\wind_{\upeta}(P)=\frac{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{P}(t_{2}))-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{P}(t_{1}))}{\upeta}.

(3.4)

In what follows, with a slight abuse of notation, if
\mathbf{P}:[t_{1},t_{2}]\to\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
is a lift of a path
P:[t_{1},t_{2}]\to\mathcal{C}_{\upeta}
, then we may write
\wind_{\upeta}(\mathbf{P})
to denote
\wind_{\upeta}(P)
.

We are now ready to state the main result of this subsection.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
X^{\mu_{a}}
and
\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{\mu_{a}}
be as specified above. For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
[a_{N}]_{0}\subset(0,1)
be an admissible sequence of height coordinates for the random walk
X^{\mu_{a}}
as specified in Definition
3.13
. Then it holds that

\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\wind_{\upeta}\bigl(\dot{\mathbf{X}}|_{[0,N]}\bigr)\mid\bigl\{\mathfrak{h}(X_{n})=a_{n}\bigr\}_{n=1}^{N}\bigr]=0.

Heuristically speaking, the proof of the above lemma goes as follows. First, we observe that, by definition of winding, it holds that

\wind_{\upeta}(\dot{\mathbf{X}}|_{[0,N]})=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\wind_{\upeta}(\dot{\mathbf{X}}|_{[j,j+1]}).

In particular, this implies that we can reduce the problem to studying the expected winding at each time step of the random walk. Suppose for a moment that
N=1
in the lemma statement. Then we only need to prove that the random walk started uniformly at random from height
a
and conditioned to hit height
a_{1}
at its first time step has zero expected winding. The reason why this is true is due to the fact that the re-randomization procedure that takes place inside each small segment ensures that the conditional hitting distribution of the segment at height
a_{1}
is also uniformly distributed. To extend the argument to the general case
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we just need to use Lemma
3.14
.

Proof of Lemma
3.17
.

We start by finding an equivalent condition under which the relation stated in the lemma holds true.
To this end, we observe that, thanks to the Definition
3.16
of winding, it holds that

\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\wind_{\upeta}\bigl(\dot{\mathbf{X}}|_{[0,N]}\bigr)\mid\bigl\{\mathfrak{h}(X_{n})=a_{n}\bigr\}_{n=1}^{N}\bigr]=\frac{1}{\upeta}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathbf{X}}_{j+1})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathbf{X}}_{j})\mid\bigl\{\mathfrak{h}(X_{n})=a_{n}\bigr\}_{n=1}^{N}\bigr],

and so the claim follows if we prove that

\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathbf{X}}_{j+1})\mid\bigl\{\mathfrak{h}(X_{n})=a_{n}\bigr\}_{n=1}^{N}\bigr]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathbf{X}}_{j})\mid\bigl\{\mathfrak{h}(X_{n})=a_{n}\bigr\}_{n=1}^{N}\bigr],\hskip 10.00002pt\forall j\in[N-1]_{0}.

An immediate application of Lemma
3.14
shows that the previous equality holds true if and only if

\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathbf{X}}_{j+1})\mid\mathfrak{h}(X_{j+1})=a_{j+1}\bigr]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathbf{X}}_{j})\mid\mathfrak{h}(X_{j})=a_{j}\bigr],\hskip 10.00002pt\forall j\in[N-1]_{0}.

(3.5)

Now, for every
k\in[N-1]_{0}
, we let
X^{k}
be the random walk on the weighted graph
(\mathcal{G},c)
started from a point in
\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a_{k})
sampled according to the probability measure
\mu_{a_{k}}
. A consequence of Lemma
3.14
is that the conditional law of
X^{\mu_{a}}
given
\mathfrak{h}(X_{k}^{\mu_{a}})=a_{k}
is equal to the law of
X^{k}
. In particular, this implies that (
3.5
) is equivalent to the fact that

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{j+1}_{0})\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{j}_{0})\bigr],\hskip 10.00002pt\forall j\in[N-1]_{0},

(3.6)

where, for
k\in[N-1]_{0}
,
\smash{\mathbf{X}^{k}}
denotes the lift of
\smash{X^{k}}
started from a point in
\smash{\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger}}_{a_{k}}}
, and
\smash{\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{k}}
is the lifted Smith-embedded random walk associated to
\mathbf{X}^{k}
. Let us also emphasize that here we are relying on the fact that the definition of winding does not depend on the particular choice of the lift.
In order to show that (
3.6
) holds true, recalling that
\upsigma_{\upeta}
is defined in (
2.15
) and denotes the covering map of the cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{\upeta}
, the equality (
3.6
) can be rewritten as follows

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\upsigma_{\upeta}(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{j+1}_{0}))\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\upsigma_{\upeta}(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{j}_{0}))\bigr],\hskip 10.00002pt\forall j\in[N-1]_{0}.

(3.7)

By construction, for every
k\in[N-1]_{0}
, the random variable
\smash{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\upsigma_{\upeta}(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{k}_{0}))}
is uniformly distributed on the interval
[0,\upeta)
, and so the result follows.
∎

4
Proof of the main result

The main goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1.4
. To this end, we fix a sequence
\{(\mathcal{G}^{n},c^{n},v_{0}^{n},v_{1}^{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of doubly marked finite weighted planar maps and we let
\{(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{n},\widehat{c}^{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be the sequence of associated weighted dual planar graphs. We assume throughout this section that such sequences satisfy hypotheses
(H1)
,
(H2)
,
(H3)
.

Before moving to the proof of the main theorem, we prove a couple of simple results which are immediate consequences of our assumptions and which will be useful later on. In particular, the next lemma is basically an analogue of assumptions
(H2)
and
(H3)
in the setting of the universal cover.

Lemma 4.1
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, view the embedded random walk on the lifted weighted graph
\smash{(\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n},c^{{\dagger},n})}
, stopped when it traverses an unbounded edge, as a continuous curve in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
obtained by piecewise linear interpolation at constant speed. For each
R>0
and for any
z\in\mathbb{R}\times[-R,R]
, the law of the random walk on
\smash{(\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n},c^{{\dagger},n})}
started from the vertex
\smash{\mathbf{x}_{z}^{n}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}}
nearest to
z
weakly converges as
n\to\infty
to the law of the Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
started from
z
with respect to the local topology on curves viewed modulo time parameterization specified in Subsection
2.1.2
, uniformly over all
z\in\mathbb{R}\times[-R,R]
. Furthermore, the same result holds for the random walk on the sequence of lifted weighted dual graphs
\smash{\{(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n},\widehat{c}^{{\dagger},n})\}_{n\in N}}
.

Proof.

Obviously, we can just prove the result for the random walk on the sequence of primal graphs as the result for the random walk on the sequence of dual graphs can be proved in the same exact way.
Fix
R>0
and
z\in\mathbb{R}\times[-R,R]
, and let
\mathbf{X}^{n,z}
be the continuous time random walk on
(\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n},c^{{\dagger},n})
started from
\mathbf{x}_{z}^{n}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}
as specified in the lemma statement. We let
\uptau_{0}:=0
and for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we define inductively

\uptau_{k+1}:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq\uptau_{k}\,:\,\mathop{Re}\nolimits(X_{t})\not\in[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(X_{\uptau_{k}})-\pi,\mathop{Re}\nolimits(X_{\uptau_{k}})+\pi)\bigr\}.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we observe that the universal covering map
\upsigma_{2\pi}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathcal{C}_{2\pi}
is a biholomorphism when restricted to
[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(X_{\uptau_{k}})-\pi,\mathop{Re}\nolimits(X_{\uptau_{k}})+\pi)
. Moreover, by assumption
(H2)
, we know that the law of
\upsigma_{2\pi}(\mathbf{X}^{n,z})
weakly converges as
n\to\infty
to the law of the Brownian motion in
\mathcal{C}_{2\pi}
with respect to the metric
\dCMPloc
specified in Subsection
2.1.2
. Therefore, since Brownian motion is conformally invariant and putting together the previous facts, we obtain that the law of the random walk
\mathbf{X}^{n,z}
weakly converges as
n\to\infty
to the law of the Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with respect to the metric
\dCMP
on the time interval
[\uptau_{k},\uptau_{k+1}]
, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Hence, the desired result follows.
∎

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we recall that
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{G}^{n}
denotes the set of faces of the graph
\mathcal{G}^{n}
. The next lemma states that, thanks to the invariance principle assumption on the sequence of weighted graphs, the maximal diameter of the faces in
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{G}^{n}
which intersect a compact subset of
\mathcal{C}_{2\pi}
is of order
o_{n}(1)
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
R>0
, and, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the set
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{G}^{n}(R)
of faces in
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{G}^{n}
that intersect
\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}\times[-R,R]
. Then, for any
\upvarepsilon>0
and for any
n>n(R,\upvarepsilon)
large enough, it holds that

\diam(f)\leq\upvarepsilon,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall f\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{G}^{n}(R).

The same result holds also for the sequence of dual graphs, i.e., with
\mathcal{F}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{n}
in place of
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{G}^{n}
.

Proof.

Obviously, we can just prove the result for the sequence of primal graphs as the result for the sequence of dual graphs can be proved in the same exact way.
We proceed by contradiction by assuming that there is
\upvarepsilon>0
such that, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a face
f_{n}\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{G}^{n}(R)
for which
\diam(f_{n})>\upvarepsilon
. We notice that each set
f_{n}\cap\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}\times[-2R,2R]
is compact and contains a path
P_{n}
with
\diam(P_{n})>\upvarepsilon
. By compactness of the Hausdorff distance
\dH
, we can assume, by possibly taking a subsequential limit, that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\dH(P_{n},P)=0
for some compact and connected set
P\subset\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}\times[-2R,2R]
.
Now, choose a rectangle
\mathrm{Q}
such that
P
disconnects the left and right sides of
\mathrm{Q}
, or the top and bottom sides of
\mathrm{Q}
. For any
n>n(R,\upvarepsilon,\mathrm{Q})
large enough, also the path
P_{n}
disconnects the rectangle
\mathrm{Q}
in the same way as
P
. Therefore, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}\times[-R,R]
, the random walk
X^{n,x}
on the weighted graph
(\mathcal{G}^{n},c^{n})
started from
x
cannot cross the rectangle
\mathrm{Q}
from left to right, or from top to bottom. However, the Brownian motion
B^{x}
on
\mathcal{C}_{2\pi}
started from
x
has positive probability to do so. This is in contradiction with assumption
(H2)
, and so the desired result follows.
∎

We are now ready to start the proof of Theorem
1.4
. We observe that this result admits a natural version on the sequence of lifted weighted graphs. Indeed, in order to prove it, we will work in the setting of the universal cover. To be more precise, we will first study in Subsection
4.1
the sequence of lifted height coordinate functions, and then, in Subsection
4.2
, we will study the sequence of lifted width coordinate functions. Once this is done, we will put everything together and we will conclude the proof of Theorem
1.4
in Subsection
4.3
.

4.1
Height coordinate function

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the lifted height coordinate function

\mathfrak{h}^{{\dagger}}_{n}:\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}\to[0,1],

as defined in (
2.4
).
The main goal of this subsection is to show that there exists an affine transformation of the function
\mathfrak{h}^{{\dagger}}_{n}
that is asymptotically close, uniformly on lifts of compact subsets of the infinite cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{2\pi}
, to the height coordinate function
\mathbf{x}\mapsto\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})
in the a priori lifted embedded graph
\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}
, as
n\to\infty
.
More precisely, we have the following result.

Proposition 4.3
.

There exit two sequences
\{b^{\mathfrak{h}}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\{c^{\mathfrak{h}}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}
such that for all
R>1
,
\updelta\in(0,1)
, and for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\bigl|c_{n}^{\mathfrak{h}}\mathfrak{h}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x})+b_{n}^{\mathfrak{h}}-\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\bigr|\leq\updelta,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(\mathbb{R}\times[-R,R]).

The proof of this proposition is given in Subsection
4.1.2
. As we will see, the proof will follow easily thanks to the harmonicity of the height coordinate function together with Lemma
4.4
below.

4.1.1
Setup of the proof

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the metric graph
\mathbb{G}^{n}
associated to
\mathcal{G}^{n}
, and let
\mathfrak{h}^{n}:\mathbb{G}^{n}\to[0,1]
be the extended height coordinate function as specified in Remark
2.7
. Given a value
S\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the set

V^{n}_{S}:=\bigl\{x\in\mathbb{G}^{n}\,:\,\mathop{Im}\nolimits(x)=S\bigr\},

and we let

\overline{a}_{S}^{n}:=\sup\left\{a\in(0,1)\,:\,\mathfrak{h}^{-1}_{n}(a)\cap V^{n}_{S}\neq\emptyset\right\},\hskip 10.00002pt\underline{a}_{S}^{n}:=\inf\left\{a\in(0,1)\,:\,\mathfrak{h}^{-1}_{n}(a)\cap V^{n}_{S}\neq\emptyset\right\}.

(4.1)

We fix throughout
R>1
and
\updelta\in(0,1)
as in the proposition statement, and we let

R^{\prime}:=\frac{R}{\updelta}.

We consider the set

W_{R,\updelta}^{n}:=\left\{V^{n}_{R^{\prime}}\cup V^{n}_{-R^{\prime}}\right\}\cup\left\{\mathfrak{h}^{-1}_{n}(\overline{a}_{R^{\prime}}^{n})\cup\mathfrak{h}^{-1}_{n}(\underline{a}_{-R^{\prime}}^{n})\right\}\subset\mathbb{G}^{n}.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, by possibly locally modifying the a priori embedding of the graph
\mathcal{G}^{n}
in the infinite cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{2\pi}
, we can assume without loss of generality that each edge in
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{n}
crosses the circles at height
R^{\prime}
and
-R^{\prime}
at most finitely many times. In particular, this implies that we can assume that the set
W_{R,\updelta}^{n}
contains at most finitely many points, and therefore, by Lemma
3.3
, we can assume, without any loss of generality, that
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}
contains all the points in
W^{n}_{R,\updelta}
.

In what follows, in order to lighten up notation, we adopt the following notational convention

\widehat{V}_{S}\equiv\widehat{V}^{n}_{S},\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{a}\equiv\overline{a}^{n}_{R^{\prime}},\hskip 10.00002pt\underline{a}\equiv\underline{a}^{n}_{-R^{\prime}}.

Furthermore, we denote by
V^{{\dagger}}_{S}
the lift to the universal cover of
V_{S}
, and we write
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(S)
(resp.
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}(S)
) as a shorthand for
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(\mathbb{R}\times[-S,S])
(resp.
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}\times[-S,S])
). We refer to Figure
7
for an illustration of the sets involved in the proof of Proposition
4.3
.

Figure 7:
A diagram illustrating the sets involved in the proof of Proposition
4.3
. The shaded dark-gray strip between heights
R
and
-R
contains all the vertices in the set
\smash{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R)}
. The shaded light-gray strip between heights
R^{\prime}
and
-R^{\prime}
contains all the vertices in the set
\smash{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime})}
. The blue vertices at the top (resp. bottom) are the vertices in the set
\smash{V^{{\dagger}}_{R^{\prime}}}
(resp.
\smash{V^{{\dagger}}_{-R^{\prime}}}
). The green vertices at the top (resp. bottom) are the vertices in the set
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})}
(resp.
(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\underline{a})
). Note that, by definition of
\overline{a}
, the set
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})}
intersects
\smash{V^{{\dagger}}_{R^{\prime}}}
, and similarly, by definition of
\underline{a}
, the set
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\underline{a})}
intersects
\smash{V^{{\dagger}}_{-R^{\prime}}}
.

Random walk notation.

For
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime})
, we consider the continuous time random walk
\{\mathbf{X}^{n,\mathbf{x}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}

on the lifted weighted graph
(\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n},c^{{\dagger},n})
started from
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime})
. We recall that the continuous path of this random walk is generated by piecewise linear interpolation at constant speed. We consider the following stopping times

\upsigma_{\mathbf{x}}:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq 0\,:\,\mathbf{X}^{n,\mathbf{x}}_{t}\in V_{R^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\cup V_{-R^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\bigr\},\hskip 10.00002pt\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq 0\,:\,\mathbf{X}^{n,\mathbf{x}}_{t}\in(\mathfrak{h}^{{\dagger}}_{n})^{-1}(\overline{a})\cup(\mathfrak{h}^{{\dagger}}_{n})^{-1}(\underline{a})\bigr\},

(4.2)

and we observe that, thanks to the definitions of
\overline{a}
and
\underline{a}
, it holds that
\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}\geq\upsigma_{\mathbf{x}}
, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime})
. Looking at Figure
7
, the stopping time
\upsigma_{\mathbf{x}}
accounts for the first time at which the random walk hits one of the blue vertices, while the stopping time
\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}
accounts for the first time at which the random walk hits one of the green vertices.

4.1.2
Proof of Proposition
4.3

We can now state a key lemma for the proof of Proposition
4.3
. The proof of the below result is given in Subsection
4.1.3
.

Lemma 4.4
.

For any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, there exists a real number
b^{\prime}_{n}=b_{n}^{\prime}(R,\updelta)
such that

\bigl|2R^{\prime}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})\bigr)-b^{\prime}_{n}-\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\bigr|\leq\updelta,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R).

Similarly, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, there exists a real number
b^{\prime\prime}_{n}=b_{n}^{\prime\prime}(R,\updelta)
such that

\bigl|2R^{\prime}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}}\in\upsigma_{2\pi}^{-1}(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{-1}(\underline{a}))\bigr)+b^{\prime\prime}_{n}+\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\bigr|\leq\updelta,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R).

Given Lemma
4.4
, the proof of Proposition
4.3
follows by using the harmonicity of the height coordinate function.

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

We divide the proof in two steps.

Step 1.
In this first step we show that, for fixed
R>1
and
\updelta\in(0,1)
, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, we can find real numbers
b^{R,\updelta}_{n}
and
c^{R,\updelta}_{n}
such that the conclusion of the proposition holds. To this end, let
R^{\prime}:=R/\updelta
, fix
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R)
and let
\mathbf{X}^{n,\mathbf{x}}
be the random walk on
(\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n},c^{{\dagger},n})
started from
\mathbf{x}
. Thanks to the harmonicity of the height coordinate function
\mathfrak{h}^{{\dagger}}_{n}
and to the optional stopping theorem, we have that

\mathfrak{h}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x})=\overline{a}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})\bigr)+\underline{a}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\underline{a})\bigr),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R).

Therefore, the problem has been reduced to proving that the probabilities appearing in the previous expressions are approximately affine transformations of the height coordinate function in the a priori embedding. By Lemma
4.4
, for all
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, there exist real numbers
b_{n}^{\prime}=b_{n}^{\prime}(R,\updelta)
and
b_{n}^{\prime\prime}=b_{n}^{\prime\prime}(R,\updelta)
for which, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\bigl|2R^{\prime}\mathfrak{h}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x})-\overline{a}(b_{n}^{\prime}+\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x}))+\underline{a}(b_{n}^{\prime\prime}+\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\leq\overline{a}\bigl|2R^{\prime}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})\bigr)-b_{n}^{\prime}-\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\bigr|+\underline{a}\bigl|2R^{\prime}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\underline{a})\bigr)+b_{n}^{\prime\prime}+\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\leq\bigl|2R^{\prime}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})\bigr)-b_{n}^{\prime}-\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\bigr|+\bigl|2R^{\prime}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\underline{a})\bigr)+b_{n}^{\prime\prime}+\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\leq\updelta.

Therefore, rearranging the terms in the above expression and letting

c^{R,\updelta}_{n}:=\frac{2R^{\prime}}{|\overline{a}-\underline{a}|},\hskip 20.00003ptb^{R,\updelta}_{n}:=\frac{\underline{a}b_{n}^{\prime\prime}-\overline{a}b_{n}^{\prime}}{|\overline{a}-\underline{a}|},

we find that, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\bigl|c^{R,\updelta}_{n}\mathfrak{h}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x})+b^{R,\updelta}_{n}-\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\bigr|\leq\updelta,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R).

Step 2.
In this second step, we show how we can define real sequences
\{b_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{c_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
independent of
R
,
\updelta
such that the conclusion of the proposition holds. To this end, consider an increasing sequence
\{R_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset[1,\infty)
and a decreasing sequence
\{\updelta_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,1)
such that
R_{k}\to\infty
and
\updelta_{k}\to 0
, as
k\to\infty
. Then, thanks to the previous step, we know that, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
n_{k}=n_{k}(R_{k},\updelta_{k})\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for all
n>n_{k}
, there exist real numbers
c_{n}^{R_{k},\updelta_{k}}
,
b_{n}^{R_{k},\updelta_{k}}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\bigl|c^{R_{k},\updelta_{k}}_{n}\mathfrak{h}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x})+b^{R_{k},\updelta_{k}}_{n}-\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\bigr|\leq\updelta_{k},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R_{k}).

Without any loss of generality, we can assume that the sequence
\{n_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is increasing. Then, for all
n\in[0,n_{1})\cap\mathbb{N}
, we let
c^{\mathfrak{h}}_{n}:=1
,
b^{\mathfrak{h}}_{n}:=1
, and for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\in[n_{k},n_{k+1})\cap\mathbb{N}
, we set
c^{\mathfrak{h}}_{n}:=c^{R_{k},\updelta_{k}}_{n}
,
b^{\mathfrak{h}}_{n}:=b^{R_{k},\updelta_{k}}_{n}
. Therefore, if we fix
R>1
and
\updelta\in(0,1)
, for all
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\bigl|c^{\mathfrak{h}}_{n}\mathfrak{h}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x})+b^{\mathfrak{h}}_{n}-\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\bigr|\leq\updelta,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R),

which concludes the proof.
∎

4.1.3
Proof of Lemma
4.4

The main goal of this subsection is prove the key Lemma
4.4
. Roughly speaking, the first estimate in Lemma
4.4
states that, for
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, the probability that the lifted random walk started inside
\smash{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R)}
hits the set
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})}
before hitting
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\underline{a})}
depends linearly on the height coordinate of the starting point of the walk on the a priori embedding. In order to prove this result, we need to rule out the possibility that the preimage of a horizontal line on the Smith embedding has large vertical fluctuations (see Figure
7
). To do so, we use the invariance principle assumption on the sequence of primal graphs, and more precisely we will follow the following steps.

1.

We start by proving that the probability that the lifted random walk started inside
\smash{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R)}
hits the set
\smash{V^{{\dagger}}_{R^{\prime}}}
before hitting
\smash{V^{{\dagger}}_{-R^{\prime}}}
depends linearly on the height coordinate of the starting point of the walk on the a priori embedding. This follows easily thanks to the invariance principle assumption and it is the content of Lemma
4.5
below.

2.

We then prove that the probability that the random walk started from
\smash{V^{{\dagger}}_{-R^{\prime}}}
has probability of order
1/R^{\prime}
to hit
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})}
before hitting
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\underline{a})}
. Once again, this is an easy consequence of the invariance principle assumption, and it is the content of Lemma
4.6
below.

3.

Finally, roughly speaking, in order to conclude, we need to improve the bound on the probability appearing in the previous step from order
1/R^{\prime}
to order
o_{n}(1/R^{\prime})
. This is done by using Lemma
2.4
together with the invariance principle assumption. Indeed, as it will be more clear in the proof of Lemma
4.4
, it is sufficient to estimate the probability that a random walk started inside
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}(R)
does not disconnect
V_{R}\cup V_{-R}
from
V_{R^{\prime}}\cup V_{-R^{\prime}}
before hitting the latter set. In Lemma
4.7
below, we will see that this probability is of order
R^{\prime}/R
, and this will be enough to conclude.

Before proceeding, we observe that, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R)
, it holds that

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}}\in\upsigma_{2\pi}^{-1}(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{-1}(\underline{a}))\bigr)=1-\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})\bigr),

hence, from now on, we can just focus on the first estimate in the statement of Lemma
4.4
.

We can now state and prove the technical lemmas mentioned above. We start with the following lemma where we study the probability that the lifted random walk started inside
\smash{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R)}
hits the set
\smash{V^{{\dagger}}_{R^{\prime}}}
before hitting
\smash{V^{{\dagger}}_{-R^{\prime}}}
.

Lemma 4.5
.

For any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\bigl|2R^{\prime}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\upsigma_{\mathbf{x}}}\in V_{R^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\bigr)-R^{\prime}-\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\bigr|\leq\updelta,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R).

Proof.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, fix a vertex
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R)
, consider a planar Brownian motion
B^{\mathbf{x}}
started from
\mathbf{x}
, and define the stopping time

\upsigma_{B,\mathbf{x}}:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq 0\,:\,\bigl|\mathop{Im}\nolimits(B^{\mathbf{x}}_{t})\bigr|=R^{\prime}\bigr\}.

Then, by assumption
(H2)
, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, we have that

\bigl|\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\upsigma_{\mathbf{x}}}\in\upsigma_{2\pi}^{-1}(V_{R^{\prime}}))-\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathop{Im}\nolimits(B_{\upsigma_{B,\mathbf{x}}})=R^{\prime}\bigr)\bigr|\leq\frac{\updelta}{2R^{\prime}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R).

Since
\mathop{Im}\nolimits(B^{\mathbf{x}})
is just a linear Brownian motion started from
\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})
, thanks to the gambler’s ruin formula, it holds that

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathop{Im}\nolimits(B_{\upsigma_{B,\mathbf{x}}})=R^{\prime}\bigr)=\frac{R^{\prime}+\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})}{2R^{\prime}},

from which the conclusion follows.
∎

We can now move to the second lemma which gives an estimate for the probability that the random walk started from
\smash{V^{{\dagger}}_{-R^{\prime}}}
hits
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})}
before hitting
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\underline{a})}
.

Lemma 4.6
.

For any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{\dagger})^{-1}(\overline{a})\bigr)\lesssim\frac{1}{R^{\prime}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in V_{-R^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}},

where the implicit constant is independent of everything else.

Proof.

We start by noticing that, for all
\mathbf{x}\in V_{-R^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}
, it holds that

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{\dagger})^{-1}(\overline{a})\bigr)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\upsigma_{2\pi}(\mathbf{X}^{n}|_{[0,\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}]})\text{ does not wind around the cylinder below height }-R^{\prime}\bigr),

where we recall that
\upsigma_{2\pi}
is defined in (
2.3
) and denotes the covering map of the infinite cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{2\pi}
. The above inequality is due to the fact that, if
\upsigma_{2\pi}(\mathbf{X}^{n}|_{[0,\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}]})
winds around the cylinder below height
-R^{\prime}
, then, by definition of
\underline{a}
, it has to hit the set
\mathfrak{h}^{-1}_{n}(\underline{a})
. We can now exploit assumption
(H2)
and find the corresponding upper bound for the Brownian motion. More precisely, let
B^{\mathbf{x}}
be a planar Brownian motion started from
\mathbf{x}\in\upsigma_{2\pi}^{-1}(V_{-R^{\prime}})
and define the stopping time

\uptau_{B,\mathbf{x}}:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq 0\,:\,\mathop{Im}\nolimits(B^{\mathbf{x}}_{t})=-2R^{\prime}\text{ or }\mathop{Im}\nolimits(B^{\mathbf{x}}_{t})=R^{\prime}\bigr\}.

Then, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\upsigma_{2\pi}(\mathbf{X}^{n}|_{[0,\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}]})\text{ does not wind around the cylinder below height }-R^{\prime}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\leq 2\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\upsigma_{2\pi}(B|_{[0,\uptau_{B,\mathbf{x}}]})\text{ does not wind around the cylinder below height }-R^{\prime}\bigr).

Therefore, to conclude, it is sufficient to find a uniform upper bound for the quantity on the right-hand side of the above expression. This is done in Lemma
A.1
from which the conclusion follows.
∎

In order to prove Lemma
4.4
, we also need the following lemma which provides an estimate for the probability that a random walk started inside
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}(R)
disconnects
V_{R}\cup V_{-R}
from
V_{R^{\prime}}\cup V_{-R^{\prime}}
before hitting the latter set.

Lemma 4.7
.

For any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\upsigma_{2\pi}(\mathbf{X}^{n}|_{[0,\upsigma_{\mathbf{x}}]})\text{ does not disconnect }V_{R}\cup V_{-R}\text{ from }V_{R^{\prime}}\cup V_{-R^{\prime}}\bigr)\lesssim\frac{R}{R^{\prime}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R),

where the implicit constant is independent of everything else.

Proof.

For
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R)
, let
B^{\mathbf{x}}
be a planar Brownian motion started from
\mathbf{x}
, and define the stopping time

\upsigma_{B,\mathbf{x}}:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq 0\,:\,|\mathop{Im}\nolimits(B_{t}^{\mathbf{x}})|=R^{\prime}\bigr\}.

By assumption
(H2)
, we know that for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\upsigma_{2\pi}(\mathbf{X}^{n}|_{[0,\upsigma_{\mathbf{x}}]})\text{ does not disconnect }V_{R}\cup V_{-R}\text{ from }V_{R^{\prime}}\cup V_{-R^{\prime}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\leq 2\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\upsigma_{2\pi}(B|_{[0,\upsigma_{B,\mathbf{x}}]})\text{ does not disconnect }\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}\times\{-R,R\}\text{ from }\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\times\{-R^{\prime},R^{\prime}\}\bigr).

Therefore, it is sufficient to find a uniform upper bound for the quantity on the right-hand side of the above expression. This is the content of Lemma
A.2
from which the desired result follows.
∎

We are now ready to give a proof of Lemma
4.4
. As we have already remarked, this will be a consequence of the previous three lemmas and of Lemma
2.4
.

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

We start by defining the function
\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}:\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime})\to\mathbb{R}
as follows

\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x}):=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})\bigr)-\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\upsigma_{\mathbf{x}}}\in V_{R^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\bigr),\hskip 20.00003pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime}),

so that, we can write

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})\bigr)=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\upsigma_{\mathbf{x}}}\in V_{R^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\bigr)+\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x}),\hskip 20.00003pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime}).

(4.3)

We now observe that, thanks to Lemma
4.5
, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\bigl|2R^{\prime}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\upsigma_{\mathbf{x}}}\in V_{R^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\bigr)-R^{\prime}-\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\bigr|\leq\updelta,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R).

(4.4)

Therefore, it is sufficient to study the function
\smash{\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}}
appearing in (
4.3
). To this end, we consider the functions
\smash{\overline{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}:\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime})\to[0,1]}
and
\smash{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}:\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime})\to[0,1]}
defined as follows

\overline{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x}):=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\upsigma_{\mathbf{x}}}\in V_{-R^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}},\,\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})\bigr),\hskip 20.00003pt\underline{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x}):=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\upsigma_{\mathbf{x}}}\in V_{R^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}},\,\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\underline{a})\bigr).

In particular, as one can easily check, it holds that

\bigl|\mathfrak{f}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{x})\bigr|\leq\overline{\mathfrak{f}}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{x})+\underline{\mathfrak{f}}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{x}),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime}),

and so, we can reduce the problem to the study of the functions
\smash{\overline{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}}
and
\smash{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}}
. We will only study the function
\smash{\overline{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}}
as the function
\smash{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}}
can be treated similarly. Thanks to the strong Markov property of the random walk
\mathbf{X}^{n,\mathbf{x}}
, we have that

\overline{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl[\overline{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\upsigma_{\mathbf{x}}})\bigr],\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime}).

Therefore, for
\mathbf{x}
,
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R)
, it holds that

\bigl|\overline{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x})-\overline{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{y})\bigr|\leq\sup\bigl\{\bigl|\overline{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{v})\bigr|\,:\,\mathbf{v}\in V_{-R^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\bigr\}\dTV\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mathbf{x}}_{\upsigma_{\mathbf{x}}},\mathbf{X}^{n,\mathbf{y}}_{\upsigma_{\mathbf{y}}}\bigr),

(4.5)

where
\dTV
denotes the total variation distance.
Hence, it is sufficient to find an upper bound for the two terms on the right-hand side of (
4.5
). We treat the two factors separately.

•

In order to bound the first factor, we just need to bound uniformly on
\smash{\mathbf{v}\in V_{-R^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}}
the probability that a random walk on
\smash{(\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n},c^{{\dagger},n})}
started from
\mathbf{v}
hits
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})}
before hitting
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\underline{a})}
. This is exactly the content of Lemma
4.6
from which we can deduce that, for all
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\sup\bigl\{\bigl|\overline{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{v})\bigr|\,:\,\mathbf{v}\in V_{-R^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\bigr\}\lesssim\frac{1}{R^{\prime}},

where the implicit constant is universal.

•

In order to bound the second factor, we can use Lemma
2.4
. More precisely, it is sufficient to estimate the probability that
\upsigma_{2\pi}(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mathbf{x}}|_{[0,\upsigma_{\mathbf{x}}]})
disconnects
V_{R}\cup V_{-R}
from
V_{R^{\prime}}\cup V_{-R^{\prime}}
. This is exactly the content of Lemma
4.7
which guarantees that, for all
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough and for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R)
, it holds that

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\upsigma_{2\pi}(\mathbf{X}^{n}|_{[0,\upsigma_{\mathbf{x}}]})\text{ does not disconnect }V_{R}\cup V_{-R}\text{ from }V_{R^{\prime}}\cup V_{-R^{\prime}}\bigr)\lesssim\frac{R}{R^{\prime}},

where the implicit constant is independent of everything else. Therefore, this fact together with Lemma
2.4
imply that

\dTV\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mathbf{x}}_{\upsigma_{\mathbf{x}}},\mathbf{X}^{n,\mathbf{y}}_{\upsigma_{\mathbf{y}}}\bigr)\lesssim\frac{R}{R^{\prime}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R).

Therefore, putting together the previous two bullet points and going back to (
4.5
), we get that for every
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\bigl|\overline{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x})-\overline{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{y})\bigr|\lesssim\frac{R}{{R^{\prime}}^{2}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R),

Furthermore, the same uniform bound can be also obtained for the function
\underline{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}
, but we omit the details since the argument is similar. Summing up, we obtained that, for all
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\bigl|\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x})-\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{y})\bigr|\lesssim\frac{R}{{R^{\prime}}^{2}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x},\,\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R).

Hence, to conclude, we can simply proceed as follows. For every
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, fix an arbitrary vertex
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R)
. Then, recalling that by definition
R^{\prime}=R/\updelta
, it holds that

2R^{\prime}|\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x})-\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{y})|\lesssim\updelta,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R).

Therefore, thanks to (
4.3
) and estimate (
4.4
), we find that, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\bigl|2R^{\prime}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\uptau_{\mathbf{x}}}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})\bigr)-b^{\prime}_{n}-\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\bigr|\leq\updelta,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R),

where
b^{\prime}_{n}:=2R^{\prime}\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{y})+R^{\prime}
.
∎

4.2
Width coordinate function

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the lifted width coordinate function

\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}_{n}:\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}\to\mathbb{R},

as defined in (
2.9
).
The main goal of this subsection is to show that there exists an affine transformation of the function
\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}_{n}
that is asymptotically close, uniformly on lifts of compact subsets of the infinite cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{2\pi}
, to the width coordinate function
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\mapsto\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})
in the a priori lifted embedded graph
\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}
, as
n\to\infty
. More precisely, we have the following result.

Proposition 4.8
.

There exits a sequence
\{b^{\mathfrak{w}}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}
such that for all
R>1
,
\updelta\in(0,1)
, and for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\left|\frac{2\pi}{\upeta_{n}}\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})+b^{\mathfrak{w}}_{n}-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})\right|\leq\updelta,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(\mathbb{R}\times[-R,R]),

(4.6)

where we recall that
\upeta_{n}
denotes the strength of the flow induced by
\mathfrak{h}_{n}
as defined in (
2.7
).

The proof of this proposition is given in Subsection
4.2.2
. As we will see, the proof is based on Lemma
4.10
below and the following two facts: (a) the harmonicity of the lifted width coordinate function
\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}_{n}
; (b) the invariance principle assumption
(H3)
on the sequence of dual maps.

4.2.1
Setup of the proof

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the metric graphs
\mathbb{G}^{n}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{n}
associated to
\mathcal{G}^{n}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{n}
, respectively. Let
\mathfrak{h}_{n}:\mathbb{G}^{n}\to[0,1]
be the extended height coordinate function as specified in Remark
2.7
, and
\mathfrak{w}_{n}:\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be the extended width coordinate function as specified in Remark
2.11
. Given a value
S\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the set

\widehat{V}^{n}_{S}:=\bigl\{\widehat{x}\in\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{n}\,:\,\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\widehat{x})=S\bigr\}.

Given a value
a\in(0,1)
, we recall that the sets
\smash{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}_{a}^{{\dagger}}}
,
\smash{\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger}}_{a}}
,
\smash{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}_{a}^{{\dagger}}}
,
\smash{\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{a}^{{\dagger}}}
are all defined in Subsection
3.2
. We also recall that
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}^{n,a}_{0}}
denotes the vertex in
\smash{\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}}
whose real coordinate is nearest to
0
, and we refer to (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) for the definitions of the sets
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{P}}^{{\dagger},n}_{a}}
and
\smash{\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{a}}
, respectively.

We fix throughout
R>1
and
\updelta\in(0,1)
as in the proposition statement, and we let

R^{\prime}:=\frac{R}{\updelta}.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we consider
\overline{a}_{R^{\prime}}^{n}
and
\underline{a}_{-R^{\prime}}^{n}
as defined in (
4.1
). Moreover, thanks to Proposition
4.3
, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, we can choose a value
a^{n}_{R^{\prime}}
such that the set
\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{-1}(a^{n}_{R^{\prime}})
is fully contained in the infinite strip
\mathbb{R}\times[-1,1]
.

In what follows, in order to lighten up notation, we adopt the following notational convention

\widehat{V}_{S}\equiv\widehat{V}^{n}_{S},\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{a}\equiv\overline{a}^{n}_{R^{\prime}},\hskip 10.00002pt\underline{a}\equiv\underline{a}^{n}_{-R^{\prime}},\hskip 10.00002pta\equiv a^{n}_{R^{\prime}}.

Furthermore, we denote by
\smash{\widehat{V}^{{\dagger}}_{S}}
the lift to the universal cover of
\smash{\widehat{V}_{S}}
, and we write
\smash{\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(S)}
(resp.
\smash{\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{n}(S)}
) as a shorthand for
\smash{\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(\mathbb{R}\times[-S,S])}
(resp.
\smash{\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{n}(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}\times[-S,S])}
).

We also adopt the convention to fix the additive constant of the lifted width coordinate function
\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}_{n}
by setting it to zero on the point
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}^{n,a}_{0}
. We observe that changing the base vertex of the lifted width coordinate function has only the effect of changing the value of
b_{n}^{\mathfrak{w}}
appearing in the statement of Proposition
4.8
.

Similarly to what we discussed in the preceding subsection, by Lemma
3.3
and Remark
3.5
, we can assume, without loss of generality, that
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}
contains all the points in the set

\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\overline{a})\cup\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{-1}(\underline{a})\cup\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{-1}(a)\subset\mathbb{G}^{n},

and that
\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{n}
contains all the points in the set

\widehat{V}_{R^{\prime}-1}\cup\widehat{V}_{-R^{\prime}+1}\subset\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{n}.

This is allowed since, as in the case of the height coordinate function, by possibly locally modifying the a priori embedding of the dual graph
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{n}
in
\mathcal{C}_{2\pi}
, we can assume that each edge in
\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{n}
crosses the circles at height
R^{\prime}-1
and
-R^{\prime}+1
at most finitely many times. We refer to Figure
8
for an illustration of the sets involved in the proof of Proposition
4.8
.

Figure 8:
A diagram illustrating the sets involved in the proof of Proposition
4.8
.
The shaded dark-gray strip between heights
R
and
-R
contains all the vertices in the set
\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R)
. The shaded light-grey strip between heights
R^{\prime}-1
and
-R^{\prime}+1
contains all the vertices in the set
\smash{\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime})}
. The red dual vertices at the top (resp. bottom) are the vertices in the set
\smash{\widehat{V}^{{\dagger}}_{R^{\prime}-1}}
(resp.
\smash{\widehat{V}^{{\dagger}}_{-R^{\prime}+1}}
). Moving from top to bottom, the blue vertices are the dual vertices in the sets
\smash{\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}}
,
\smash{\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{a}}
,
\smash{\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\underline{a}}}
; the blue dual edges are the edges in the sets
\smash{\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}}
,
\smash{\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{a}}
,
\smash{\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\underline{a}}}
; and finally the green vertices are the vertices in the sets
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})}
,
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(a)}
,
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\underline{a})}
.

Remark 4.9
.

We observe that, by Proposition
4.3
, for any
\upvarepsilon\in(0,1)
and for any
n>n(R,\updelta,\upvarepsilon)
large enough, it holds that

(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})\subset\mathbb{R}\times[R^{\prime}-\upvarepsilon,R^{\prime}+\upvarepsilon],\hskip 20.00003pt(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\underline{a})\subset\mathbb{R}\times[-R^{\prime}-\upvarepsilon,-R^{\prime}+\upvarepsilon].

Furthermore, by definitions of
\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}
,
\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\underline{a}}
and Lemma
4.2
, we have that, for any
\upvarepsilon\in(0,1)
and for any
n>n(R,\updelta,\upvarepsilon)
large enough, it holds that

\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}\subset\mathbb{R}\times[R^{\prime}-\upvarepsilon,R^{\prime}+\upvarepsilon],\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\underline{a}}\subset\mathbb{R}\times[-R^{\prime}-\upvarepsilon,-R^{\prime}+\upvarepsilon].

These facts will be of key importance in the remaining part of this subsection and they will be used several times.

Random walk notation.

For
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime}-1)}
, we consider the continuous time random walk
\smash{\{\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}\}_{t\geq 0}}
on the lifted weighted dual graph
\smash{(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n},\widehat{c}^{{\dagger},n})}
started from
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}
