. We recall that the continuous path of this random walk is simply generated by piecewise linear interpolation at constant speed. We consider the following stopping times

\upsigma_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq 0\,:\,\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}_{t}\in\widehat{V}^{{\dagger}}_{R^{\prime}-1}\cup\widehat{V}^{{\dagger}}_{-R^{\prime}+1}\bigr\},\hskip 10.00002pt\uptau_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq 0\,:\,\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}_{t}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}\cup\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\underline{a}}\bigr\}.

(4.7)

As we will observe more precisely below, for
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, thanks to Proposition
4.3
, we have that

\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}\subset\mathbb{R}\times[R^{\prime}-1,R^{\prime}+1],\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\underline{a}}\subset\mathbb{R}\times[-R^{\prime}-1,-R^{\prime}+1],

and so, it holds that
\smash{\uptau_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}\geq\upsigma_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}}
, for all
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime}-1)}
. Looking at Figure
8
, the stopping time
\smash{\upsigma_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}}
accounts for the first time at which the random walk hits one of the red dual vertices, while the stopping time
\smash{\uptau_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}}
accounts for the first time at which the random walk hits one of the blue dual vertices at the top or bottom of the figure.

In what follows, we also need to consider random walks on the sequence of primal lifted weighted graphs. To be precise, let
a
be as specified in the introduction of this section (we recall that here,
a
is a shorthand for
a_{R^{\prime}}^{n}
) and consider the probability measure
\mu_{a}
on the set
\smash{\mathfrak{h}^{-1}(a)}
as specified in Definition
3.11
. We let
\smash{X^{n,\mu_{a}}}
be the random walk on the weighted graph
(\mathcal{G}^{n},c^{n})
started from a point in
\smash{\mathfrak{h}^{-1}_{n}(a)}
sampled according to
\mu_{a}
. We also consider the associated continuous time lifted random walk
\smash{\{\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}}
on
\smash{(\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n},c^{{\dagger},n})}
started from a point in
\smash{\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{a}}
sampled according to
\mu_{a}
. We define the following stopping times

\upvartheta_{+}:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq 0\,:\,\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{t}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})\bigr\},\hskip 10.00002pt\upvartheta_{-}:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq 0\,:\,\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{t}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\underline{a})\bigr\}.

(4.8)

Furthermore, we will also need to consider the lifted Smith-embedded random walk
\smash{\{\dot{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{n,\mu_{a}}\}_{t\geq 0}}
associated to
\smash{\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}}
defined in Subsection
3.4
.

4.2.2
Proof of Proposition
4.8

We can now state a key lemma for the proof of Proposition
4.8
. The proof of the below result is given in Subsection
4.2.3
.

Lemma 4.10
.

For any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough there is a finite constant
b^{\prime}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}\left[\frac{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\uptau_{\widehat{x}}})}{2\pi}-\frac{\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\uptau_{\widehat{x}}})+b^{\prime}_{n}}{\upeta_{n}}\right]\right|\lesssim 1,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime}-1),

where the implicit constant is universal, and
\upeta_{n}
denotes the strength of the flow induced by
\mathfrak{h}_{n}
as defined in (
2.7
).

Given Lemma
4.10
, the proof of Proposition
4.8
follows by using the harmonicity of the width coordinate function and the invariance principle assumption
(H3)
.

Proof of Proposition
4.8
.

For
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R)}
, let
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}}
be the random walk on the lifted dual weighted graph
\smash{(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n},\widehat{c}^{{\dagger},n})}
started from
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}
. Moreover, let
B^{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}
be a planar Brownian motion started from
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}
, and define the stopping time

\uptau_{B,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq 0\,:\,|\mathop{Im}\nolimits(B_{t}^{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}})|=R^{\prime}\bigr\}.

(4.9)

We divide the proof into several steps.

Step 1.

In this first step, we show that, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\uptau_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}})\bigr]-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})\bigr|\leq\updelta,

(4.10)

where we recall that the stopping time
\uptau_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}
is defined in (
4.7
). As we will see below, this is an easy consequence of assumption
(H3)
.
We start by observing that, thanks to well-known properties of Brownian motion, it holds that

\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(B_{\uptau_{B,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}})]-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})\bigr|=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R).

Indeed, this follows from the fact that
\smash{|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(B_{\uptau_{B,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}})]-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})|}
has exponentially decaying tails and from the optional stopping theorem. As we observed in Remark
4.9
, we have that, for any
\upvarepsilon\in(0,1)
and for any
n>n(R,\updelta,\upvarepsilon)
large enough, it holds that

\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}\subset\mathbb{R}\times[R^{\prime}-\upvarepsilon,R^{\prime}+\upvarepsilon],\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\underline{a}}\subset\mathbb{R}\times[-R^{\prime}-\upvarepsilon,-R^{\prime}+\upvarepsilon].

Therefore, from this fact and assumption
(H3)
, we can deduce that, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(B_{\uptau_{B,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}})\bigr]-\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\uptau_{\widehat{x}}})\bigr]\bigr|\leq\updelta.

More precisely, this fact can be obtained from assumption
(H3)
by arguing in the same exact way as in the proof of Lemma
4.12
below.

Step 2.
The main goal of this step is to prove that, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}\bigl[\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{{\dagger}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\widehat{\uptau}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}}\bigr)\bigr]=\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}}),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R).

(4.11)

We start by recalling that the function
\smash{\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{{\dagger}}:\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}\to\mathbb{R}}
is harmonic, and so the process
\smash{\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}})}
is a discrete martingale with respect to the filtration generated by
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}}
. Therefore, if we prove that this martingale is uniformly integrable, then the claim follows from the optional stopping theorem. To this end, it is sufficient to prove that, for
M\in\mathbb{N}
, the probability of the event
\smash{|\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}_{\widehat{\uptau}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}})-\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})|>MR^{\prime}}
decays exponentially fast in
M
, uniformly in
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R)}
and for all
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough. This fact can be obtained from assumption
(H3)
by arguing in the same exact way as in the proof of Lemma
4.11
below. Hence, the desired result follows.

Step 3.
Consider the function
\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}:\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime}-1)\to\mathbb{R}
defined as follows

\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}}):=\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}\left[\frac{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\uptau_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}})}{2\pi}-\frac{\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\uptau_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}})+b^{\prime}_{n}}{\upeta_{n}}\right],\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime}-1),

where
b^{\prime}_{n}
is the same constant appearing in the statement of Lemma
4.10
.
Now, recalling the definition (
4.7
) of the stopping time
\upsigma_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}
and that
\upsigma_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}\leq\uptau_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}
, thanks to the strong Markov property of the random walk, for all
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}
,
\widehat{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R)
, it holds that

\bigl|\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})-\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{y}})\bigr|\leq\sup\bigl\{\bigl|\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{v}})\bigr|\,:\,{\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime}-1)}\bigr\}\dTV\bigl(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}_{\upsigma_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}},\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\upsigma_{\widehat{\mathbf{y}}}}\bigr),

(4.12)

where
\dTV
denotes the total variation distance.
Hence, it is sufficent to find an upper bound for the two terms on the right-hand side of (
4.12
). We treat the two factors separately.

•

In order to bound the first factor, we just need to bound uniformly on
\smash{\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime}-1)}
the quantity
\smash{|\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{v})|}
. This is exactly the content of Lemma
4.10
from which we can deduce that, for all
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\sup\bigl\{\bigl|\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{v}})\bigr|\,:\,{\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime}-1)}\bigr\}\lesssim 1,

where the implicit constant is independent of everything else.

•

In order to bound the second factor, we can use Lemma
2.4
. Indeed, as we have already remarked, thanks to Proposition
4.3
and to Lemma
4.2
, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
large enough, it holds that

\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\bigl[R^{\prime}-1,R^{\prime}+1\bigr],\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\underline{a}}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\bigl[-R^{\prime}-1,-R^{\prime}+1\bigr].

Therefore, it is sufficient to estimate the probability that
\smash{\upsigma_{2\pi}(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}|_{[0,\upsigma_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}]})}
disconnects
\smash{\widehat{V}_{R}\cup\widehat{V}_{-R}}
from
\smash{\widehat{V}_{R^{\prime}-1}\cup\widehat{V}_{-R^{\prime}+1}}
. To this end, one can argue in the same exact way as in Lemma
4.7
in order to prove that, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough and for all
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R)}
, it holds that

\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}(\upsigma_{2\pi}(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n}|_{[0,\widehat{\upsigma}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}]})\text{ does not disconnect }\widehat{V}_{R}\cup\widehat{V}_{-R}\text{ from }\widehat{V}_{R^{\prime}-1}\cup\widehat{V}_{-R^{\prime}+1})\lesssim\frac{R}{R^{\prime}},

where the implicit constant is independent of everything else. Therefore, this fact together with Lemma
2.4
imply that

\dTV\bigl(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\widehat{x}}_{\widehat{\upsigma}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}},\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{y}}}_{\widehat{\upsigma}_{\widehat{y}}}\bigr)\lesssim\frac{R}{R^{\prime}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\widehat{\mathbf{x}},\widehat{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R).

Therefore, putting together the two bullet points above, recalling that
R^{\prime}=R/\updelta
, and going back to (
4.12
), we find that, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\bigl|\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})-\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{y}})\bigr|\lesssim\updelta,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\widehat{\mathbf{x}},\,\widehat{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R),

(4.13)

where the implicit constant is independent of everything else.

Step 4.

To conclude, for every
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, fix an arbitrary vertex
\widehat{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R)
. Then, thanks to (
4.10
), (
4.11
), and (
4.13
), we have that for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\left|\frac{2\pi}{\upeta_{n}}\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})+b^{R,\updelta}_{n}-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})\right|\leq\updelta,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R),

where
b^{R,\updelta}_{n}:=2\pi\mathfrak{f}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{y}})
. Finally, in order to conclude, we need to remove the dependence of
b^{R,\updelta}_{n}
from
R
and
\updelta
. This can be easily done by arguing in the same exact way as in the second step of the proof of Proposition
4.3
. Therefore, the proof is concluded.
∎

4.2.3
Proof of Lemma
4.10

The proof of Lemma
4.10
is relatively long and it is based on several intermediate technical lemmas.

We recall that, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the map
\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}_{n}
, defined in Definition
2.15
, assigns to each vertex
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}
the random variable
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{n}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}(\mathbf{x})
, which is uniformly distributed on the horizontal segment
\mathcal{S}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{x})
. The proof of Lemma
4.10
can be basically divided into four main steps.

1.

We start by considering the continuous time lifted random walk
\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}
. In Lemma
4.11
, we prove that, for
M\in\mathbb{N}
, the conditional probability, given
\smash{\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}}
, of the event
\smash{|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{0})|>MR^{\prime}}
decays exponentially in
M
. Using this result, we then prove in Lemma
4.12
that the conditional expectation of
\smash{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{0})}
, given
\smash{\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}}
, is of order one. Both the proofs of Lemmas
4.11
and
4.12
are based on the invariance principle assumption on the sequence of primal maps.

2.

We then consider the Smith-embedded random walk
\smash{\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\mu_{a}}}
associated with
\smash{\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}}
. In Lemma
4.13
, we prove that the conditional expectation of
\smash{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}})}
, given
\smash{\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}}
, is of order
\upeta_{n}
. This result is basically a consequence of Lemma
3.17
, which guarantees that the expected horizontal winding of the Smith-embedded random walk is equal to zero.

3.

In Lemma
4.15
, using the relation between the maps
\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}_{n}
, together with the results proved in the previous steps, we prove that there is a finite constant
b_{n}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
such that the values of the width coordinate function in
\smash{\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}}
plus
\smash{b^{\prime}_{n}}
is of order
\upeta_{n}
. The key input to prove this result comes from Lemma
4.14
in which we prove that the probability that the random walk
\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}
travels a large horizontal distance in a narrow horizontal tube decays exponentially fast. The proof of this fact follows from the invariance principle assumption on the sequence of primal maps.

4.

Finally, in Lemma
4.16
, we state the analogous dual result of Lemma
4.14
. This fact and the periodicity of the Smith diagram will allow us to deduce Lemma
4.10
.

Let us emphasize that all the results explained above hold also with the role of
\upvartheta_{+}
and
\upvartheta_{-}
interchanged. In particular, the same result stated in point (c) holds with the set
\smash{\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}}
replaced by
\smash{\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{\underline{a}}}
.

We can now proceed to state and prove the technical lemmas mentioned above. We start with the following lemma which states that, for
M\in\mathbb{N}
, the conditional probability of the event
\smash{|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{0})|>MR^{\prime}}
, given
\smash{\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}}
, decays exponentially in
M
. Heuristically speaking, this is due to the fact that, after each time that the random walk
\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}
travels horizontal distance
R^{\prime}
, there is a positive chance of hitting the set
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})}
.

Lemma 4.11
.

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\upvartheta_{+}})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{0})|>MR^{\prime}\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr)\lesssim\exp(-CM),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall M\in\mathbb{N}.

where the implicit constant is independent of everything else. The same conclusion holds with the role of
\upvartheta_{+}
and
\upvartheta_{-}
interchanged.

Proof.

As observed in Remark
4.9
, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, we can assume that

(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})\subset\mathbb{R}\times[R^{\prime},R^{\prime}+1],\hskip 10.00002pt(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\underline{a})\subset\mathbb{R}\times[-R^{\prime}-1,-R^{\prime}],\hskip 10.00002pt(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(a)\subset\mathbb{R}\times[-1,1].

(4.14)

Now, letting
\upvartheta:=\upvartheta_{+}\wedge\upvartheta_{-}
and
M\in\mathbb{N}
, we can write

\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\upvartheta_{+}})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{0})|>MR^{\prime}\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr)\leq\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\upvartheta})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{0})|>MR^{\prime}\bigr)}{\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr)}.

We note that, thanks to assumption
(H2)
and (
4.14
), for any
\smash{n>n(R,\updelta)}
large enough, the probability on the denominator can be lower bounded by a constant independent of everything else. Therefore, we can just focus on the probability appearing on the numerator. To this end, let
\uprho_{0}:=0
, and for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
define inductively

\uprho_{k+1}:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq\uprho_{k}\,:\,\bigl|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{t})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\uprho_{k}})\bigr|\geq R^{\prime}\bigr\}.

Moreover, for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, consider the event

A^{n}_{k}:=\bigl\{\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}|_{[\uprho_{k},\uprho_{k+1}]}\not\subset\mathbb{R}\times\bigl[\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\uprho_{k}})-3R^{\prime},\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\uprho_{k}})+3R^{\prime}\bigr]\bigr\}.

We observe that, thanks to the strong Markov property of the random walk, the events
\{A^{n}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
are independent and identically distributed. Moreover, thanks to assumption
(H2)
, to estimates (
4.14
), and to well-known properties of Brownian motion, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
large enough, the event
A^{n}_{0}
happens with uniformly positive probability
p
independent of everything else.
Therefore, we have that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\upvartheta_{+}})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{0})|>MR^{\prime}\bigr)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\left(\bigcap_{i=0}^{M}\overline{A^{n}_{i}}\right)=(1-p)^{M},

from which the desired result follows. Finally, the same argument also applies with the role of
\upvartheta_{+}
and
\upvartheta_{-}
interchanged.
∎

In the next lemma, we use Lemma
4.11
to prove that the conditional expectation of
\smash{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{0})}
, given
\smash{\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}}
, is of order one.

Lemma 4.12
.

For any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\upvartheta_{+}})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{0})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr]\bigr|\lesssim 1,

where the implicit constant is universal. The same result holds with the role of
\upvartheta_{+}
and
\upvartheta_{-}
interchanged.

Proof.

The proof of this result is based on assumption
(H2)
. More precisely, let
B^{\mu_{a}}
be a planar Brownian motion started from a point in
\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{a}
sampled according to the probability measure
\mu_{a}
, and consider the following stopping times

\upvartheta_{B,+}:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq 0\,:\,\mathop{Im}\nolimits(B_{t}^{\mu_{a}})=R^{\prime}\bigr\},\hskip 20.00003pt\upvartheta_{B,-}:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq 0\,:\,\mathop{Im}\nolimits(B_{t}^{\mu_{a}})=-R^{\prime}\bigr\}.

Since
\mathop{Re}\nolimits(B^{\mu_{a}})
and
\mathop{Im}\nolimits(B^{\mu_{a}})
are independent, and since the stopping times
\upvartheta_{B,+}
and
\upvartheta_{B,-}
only depend on
\mathop{Im}\nolimits(B^{\mu_{a}})
, thanks to well-known properties of Brownian motion, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(B_{\upvartheta_{B,+}})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(B_{0})\mid\upvartheta_{B,+}<\upvartheta_{B,-}\bigr]=0.

(4.15)

Furthermore, as observed in Remark
4.9
, for any
\upvarepsilon\in(0,1)
and for any
n>n(R,\updelta,\upvarepsilon)
large enough, it holds that

(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})\subset\mathbb{R}\times[R^{\prime},R^{\prime}+\upvarepsilon],\hskip 20.00003pt(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\underline{a})\subset\mathbb{R}\times[-R^{\prime}-\upvarepsilon,-R^{\prime}].

In particular, this fact and Lemma
4.2
imply that the set
(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})
(resp.
(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\underline{a})
) converges in the Hausdorff metric to the horizontal line
\mathbb{R}\times\{R^{\prime}\}
(resp.
\mathbb{R}\times\{-R^{\prime}\}
). Therefore, thanks to assumption
(H2)
, the following weak convergence of laws holds

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{Law}\bigl(\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(X^{n,\mu_{a}}_{0})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr)=\mathrm{Law}\bigl(\mathop{Re}\nolimits(B^{\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{B,+}})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(B^{\mu_{a}}_{0})\mid\upvartheta_{B,+}<\upvartheta_{B,-}\bigr).

Hence, thanks to Lemma
4.11
and Vitali’s convergence theorem, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\upvartheta_{+}})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{0})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr]-\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(B_{\upvartheta_{B,+}})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(B_{0})\mid\upvartheta_{B,+}<\upvartheta_{B,-}\bigr]\bigr|\leq 1.

(4.16)

Hence, putting together (
4.15
) and (
4.16
), we obtain the desired result. Finally, the same argument also applies with the role of
\upvartheta_{+}
and
\upvartheta_{-}
interchanged.
∎

In the next lemma, we see how we can use Lemma
3.17
to prove that that the conditional expectation of
\smash{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}})}
, given
\smash{\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}}
, is of order
\upeta_{n}
.

Lemma 4.13
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, it holds that

\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\upvartheta_{+}})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr]\bigr|\leq\upeta_{n},

and the same with the role of
\upvartheta_{+}
and
\upvartheta_{-}
interchanged.

Proof.

Since we are assuming that the base vertex
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}^{n,a}_{0}
of the lifted width coordinate function
\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}_{n}
belongs to the set
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}^{{\dagger},n}_{a}
, then, thanks to Lemma
3.9
, it holds almost surely that

\bigl|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}_{n}(\mathbf{x}))\bigr|\leq\upeta_{n},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{a}.

In particular, since the embedded random walk
\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}
is started from a point in the set
\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{a}
, then it holds almost surely that

\bigl|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{0})\bigr|\leq\upeta_{n}.

(4.17)

Now, we would like to apply Lemma
3.17
in order to conclude. However, we note that we cannot directly apply this result since, a priori, it does not hold that
\cup_{x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}}\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{-1}(\mathfrak{h}_{n}(x))\subseteq\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}
. In order to overcome this issue, we could consider the weighted graph associated to
(\mathcal{G}^{n},c^{n})
and
\cup_{x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}}\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{-1}(\mathfrak{h}_{n}(x))
as specified in Definition
3.1
. We could then apply Lemma
3.17
to the random walk on this new weighted graph and then transfer the result to the original weighted graph by means of Lemma
3.4
. In order to lighten up the proof, we will assume directly that
\cup_{x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}}\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{-1}(\mathfrak{h}_{n}(x))=\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}
. Since the stopping time
\upvartheta_{+}
is almost surely finite, we can proceed as follows

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\wind_{\upeta_{n}}(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{n}|_{[0,\upvartheta_{+}]})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr]

(4.18)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}(\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-})}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\left[\sum_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\wind_{\upeta_{n}}(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{n}|_{[0,\upvartheta_{+}]})\mathbbm{1}_{\{\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-},\upvartheta_{+}=N\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}(\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-})}\sum_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\wind_{\upeta_{n}}(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{n}|_{[0,\upvartheta_{+}]})\mid\upvartheta_{-}>N,\upvartheta_{+}=N\bigr]\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}(\upvartheta_{-}>N,\upvartheta_{+}=N).

In order to pass from the first line to the second line of the above expression, we used the fact that

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl|\wind_{\upeta_{n}}(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\mu_{a}}|_{[0,\upvartheta_{+}]})\bigr|\bigr]<\infty,

(4.19)

and Fubini’s theorem. The reason why (
4.19
) holds is an immediate consequence of Lemmas
3.6
and
4.12
. In order to conclude, it is sufficient to prove that the expectation in each summand of the sum appearing in (
4.18
) is equal to zero. To this end, fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
and consider a sequence of admissible height coordinates
[a_{N}]_{0}\subset(0,1)
for the random walk
X^{\mu_{a}}
, as specified in Definition
3.13
, such that
a_{m}>\underline{a}
for all
m\in[N]_{0}
and
a_{N}=\overline{a}
. Thanks, to Lemma
3.17
, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\wind_{\upeta_{n}}(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{n}|_{[0,N]})\mid\{\mathfrak{h}_{n}(X_{m})=a_{m}\}_{m=1}^{N}\bigr]=0,

uniformly over all such sequences of height coordinates. Hence, this is sufficient to conclude that

\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\wind_{\upeta_{n}}(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{n}|_{[0,\upvartheta_{+}]})\mid\upvartheta_{-}>N,\upvartheta_{+}=N\bigr]=0.

(4.20)

Therefore, the conclusion follows thanks to (
4.17
), and to the fact that (
4.18
) and (
4.20
) imply that
\smash{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}[\wind_{\upeta_{n}}(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{n}|_{[0,\upvartheta_{+}]})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}]=0}
. Finally, we observe that the same argument also applies with the role of
\upvartheta_{+}
and
\upvartheta_{-}
interchanged.
∎

In order to prove Lemma
4.10
, we basically need to prove that the difference between the value of the width coordinate in the set
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{P}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}}
and in the set
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{P}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\underline{a}}}
is of order
\upeta_{n}
. This fact is the content of Lemma
4.15
below. However, in order to prove this fact, we first need to prove that it is extremely unlikely for the random walk
\smash{\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}}
to travel a large horizontal distance in a “narrow horizontal tube”. We refer to Figure
9
for a diagram illustrating the various objects involved in the proof of Lemma
4.14
.

Figure 9:
The vertices
(\mathfrak{h}^{{\dagger}}_{n})^{-1}(\overline{a})
are drawn for simplicity as a continuous red/green path contained in the infinite strip
\mathbb{R}\times[R^{\prime}-\upvarepsilon,R^{\prime}+\upvarepsilon]
. In particular, the green part of this path contains the vertices in the set
\smash{\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}}
. The blue vertex at the bottom of the figure is a vertex sampled from the set
\smash{\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{a}}
according to the probability measure
\mu_{a}
. The blue path represents a trajectory of the random walk
\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}
, and the blue point at the top-right coincides with the hitting point
\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}}
. The blue point
\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}}
at the top-left is the vertex in
\smash{\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}}
such that
\smash{\upsigma_{2\pi}(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}})=\upsigma_{2\pi}(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}})}
. Roughly speaking, Lemma
4.14
says that the event depicted in the figure is extremely unlikely to happen. A consequence of this fact is that the conditional expectation of
\smash{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}})}
, given
\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}
, is of order one.

Lemma 4.14
.

Fix
\upvarepsilon\in(0,1)
,
M\in\mathbb{N}
, and define the following event

\mathrm{A}_{M,\upvarepsilon}^{n,+}:=\bigl\{\exists\,s,t\in[0,\upvartheta_{+}]\,:\,\bigl|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{t})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{s})\bigr|>M;\;\bigl|\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{u})\bigr|\in[R^{\prime}-\upvarepsilon,R^{\prime}+\upvarepsilon],\;\forall u\in[s,t]\bigr\}.

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that, for any
n>n(R,\updelta,\upvarepsilon)
large enough, it holds that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(\mathrm{A}_{M,\upvarepsilon}^{n,+}\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr)\lesssim\exp(-CM/\upvarepsilon),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall M\in\mathbb{N},

where the implicit constant is independent of everything else. Furthermore, the same conclusion holds with the role of
\upvartheta_{+}
and
\upvartheta_{-}
interchanged.

Proof.

Fix
\upvarepsilon\in(0,1)
. We start by recalling that, as observed in Remark
4.9
, for any
n>n(R,\updelta,\upvarepsilon)
large enough, it holds that

(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})\subset\mathbb{R}\times[R^{\prime}-\upvarepsilon,R^{\prime}+\upvarepsilon].

(4.21)

It is easy to see that, without any loss of generality, we can assume that
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}
contains all the points in the set

\bigl\{x\in\mathbb{G}^{n}\,:\,\mathop{Im}\nolimits(x)=R^{\prime}-\upvarepsilon\bigr\}.

Therefore, we can define the stopping time
\uprho_{0}:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq 0\,:\,\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{t})=R^{\prime}-\upvarepsilon\}
, and for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we define inductively

\widetilde{\uprho}_{k+1}:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq\uprho_{k}\,:\,\bigl|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{t})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\uprho_{k}})\bigr|>M\},\hskip 10.00002pt\uprho_{k+1}:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq\widetilde{\uprho}_{k+1}\,:\,\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{t})=R^{\prime}-\upvarepsilon\bigr\}.

Moreover, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
we consider the events

\mathrm{A}_{M,\upvarepsilon}^{n,+}(\uprho_{k-1},\widetilde{\uprho}_{k}):=\bigl\{\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}|_{[\uprho_{k-1},\widetilde{\uprho}_{k}]})\subset[R^{\prime}-\upvarepsilon,R^{\prime}+\upvarepsilon]\bigr\},\hskip 10.00002ptF_{k}:=\bigl\{\uprho_{k}>\widehat{\uptau}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}\bigr\}.

Let
K
be the smallest
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
F_{k}
occurs. Then, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(\mathrm{A}_{M,\upvarepsilon}^{n,+}\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{K}\mathrm{A}_{M,\upvarepsilon}^{n,+}(\uprho_{i-1},\widetilde{\uprho}_{i})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\right)

(4.22)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\left(\mathrm{A}_{M,\upvarepsilon}^{n,+}(\uprho_{i-1},\widetilde{\uprho}_{i})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\right)\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(K=k\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr).

Thanks to the strong Markov property of the random walk, the events
\{\mathrm{A}_{M,\upvarepsilon}^{n,+}(\uprho_{k-1},\widetilde{\uprho}_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are conditionally independent and identically distributed given
\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}
.
Now, thanks to assumption
(H2)
and well-known properties of Brownian motion, it is possible to prove that there is a universal constant
C>0
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
large enough it holds that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(\mathrm{A}_{M,\upvarepsilon}^{n,+}(\uprho_{0},\widetilde{\uprho}_{1})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr)\lesssim\exp\bigl(-CM/\upvarepsilon\bigr),

(4.23)

where the implicit constant is independent of everything else. More precisely, in order to obtain the above upper bound, it is sufficient to study the probability that the random walk travels horizontal distance
M
before exiting an infinite horizontal band of height of order
\upvarepsilon
. This can be done by proceeding similarly to the proof of Lemma
4.11
, and so we do not detail the argument here. Now, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we consider the event

\mathrm{B}_{M}^{n,+}(\uprho_{k-1},\widetilde{\uprho}_{k}):=\bigl\{\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}|_{[\uprho_{k-1},\widetilde{\uprho}_{k}]})\subset[-R^{\prime}-1,R^{\prime}+1]\bigr\}.

For the same reason explained above, we have that the events
\{\mathrm{B}_{M}^{n,+}(\uprho_{k-1},\widetilde{\uprho}_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are conditionally independent and identically distributed given
\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}
. Also, using again assumption
(H2)
and a standard gambler’s ruin estimate for Brownian motion, one can prove that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(\mathrm{B}_{M}^{n,+}(\uprho_{0},\widetilde{\uprho}_{1})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr)\lesssim(M+1)^{-1},

(4.24)

where the implicit constant is independent of everything else. In particular, thanks to (
4.21
), for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(K=k\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathrm{B}_{M}^{n,+}(\uprho_{i-1},\widetilde{\uprho}_{i})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(\mathrm{B}_{M}^{n,+}(\uprho_{0},\widetilde{\uprho}_{1})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr)

\displaystyle\lesssim(M+1)^{-(k-1)}.

Therefore, putting together (
4.22
), (
4.23
) and (
4.24
), we obtain that there is a universal constant
C>0
such that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl(\mathrm{A}_{M,\upvarepsilon}^{n,+}\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr)\lesssim\exp\bigl(-CM/\upvarepsilon\bigr)\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k(M+1)^{-(k-1)}\lesssim\exp\bigl(-CM/\upvarepsilon\bigr).

Finally, we observe that the result with the role of
\upvartheta^{+}
and
\upvartheta^{-}
interchanged can be obtained in the same way. Therefore, this concludes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to prove that the difference between the value of the width coordinate in the set
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{P}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}}
and in the set
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{P}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\underline{a}}}
is of order
\upeta_{n}
. We refer to Figure
10
for a diagram illustrating the various objects involved in the proof of Lemma
4.15
.

Figure 10:
A diagram illustrating Step 2 of the proof of Lemma
4.15
.
Left:
A portion of the lifted graph
\smash{\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}}
embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The blue/green vertices are the vertices in
\smash{\mathcal{P}_{\overline{a}}^{\dagger,n}}
. The blue edges are the edges in the set
\smash{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{a}}^{{\dagger},n}}
. The red dual edges are the edges in the set
\smash{\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}}
. The red dual vertices are the vertices in the set
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{P}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}}
. Consider the dual vertex
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}
in
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{P}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}}
, then the two red dual edges with an arrow are the edges
\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
,
\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
associated to
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}
. The blue edges with an arrow are the corresponding primal edges
\mathbf{e}_{1}
,
\mathbf{e}_{2}
. The two green points are the endpoints of
\mathbf{e}_{1}
,
\mathbf{e}_{2}
in
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}(\overline{a})}
.
Right:
A portion of the tiling of
\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
constructed via the lifted tiling map
\smash{\mathcal{S}_{n}^{{\dagger}}}
. The blue horizontal line corresponds to
\smash{\cup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}}\mathcal{S}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x})}
. The two green points are possible realizations of
\smash{\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{n}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}(\mathbf{x}_{1})}
and
\smash{\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{n}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}(\mathbf{x}_{2})}
. The vertical red segment with horizontal coordinate
\smash{\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})}
corresponds to
\smash{\mathcal{S}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})}
.

Lemma 4.15
.

For any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, there is a finite constant
b^{\prime}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\bigl|\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})+b_{n}^{\prime}\bigr|\lesssim\upeta_{n},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{P}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{P}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\underline{a}},

where the implicit constant is independent of everything else.

Proof.

We start by letting
\upvartheta:=\upvartheta_{+}\wedge\upvartheta_{-}
and defining

b_{n}^{\prime}:=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{0})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\upvartheta})\bigr].

We observe that
b_{n}^{\prime}
is not of constant order in general. Indeed, this is due to the fact that
\smash{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{0})}
can be far from being of order one since the starting point of the random walk
\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}
is sampled from the set
\smash{\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{a}}
over which we do not have any a priori control. We will only prove the result in the case
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{P}}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}}
since the result for
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\smash{\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{\underline{a}}}
can be obtained similarly. In particular, we split the proof in two steps.

Step 1:
In this first step, we claim that for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds almost surely that

\bigl|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}_{n}(\mathbf{x}))+b_{n}^{\prime}\bigr|\lesssim\upeta_{n},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}},

where the implicit constant does not depend on anything else. To this end, we consider the vertex
\smash{\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}}\in\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}}
such that
\smash{\upsigma_{2\pi}(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}})=\upsigma_{2\pi}(\mathbf{X}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}})}
. Now, applying Lemma
3.6
, we see that

\left|\frac{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}_{n}(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}}))-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}})}{\upeta_{n}}-\frac{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits({\mathbf{X}}^{n,\mu_{a}}_{\upvartheta_{+}})}{2\pi}\right|\leq 1.

(4.25)

Therefore, rearranging the various terms in the previous inequality and then taking the conditional expectation given
\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}
, we get that

\displaystyle\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}_{n}(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\upvartheta_{+}}))\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr]+b_{n}^{\prime}\bigr|

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\upvartheta_{+}})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr]\bigr|

(4.26)

\displaystyle+\upeta_{n}\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\upvartheta_{+}})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{0})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr]\bigr|

\displaystyle+\upeta_{n}\bigl|b_{n}^{\prime}+\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\upvartheta_{+}})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}_{0}^{n})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr]\bigr|.

Thanks to Lemmas
4.13
and
4.12
, we have that the following inequalities hold for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough

\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\upvartheta_{+}})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr]\bigr|\leq\upeta_{n},\hskip 20.00003pt\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\upvartheta_{+}})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{X}^{n}_{0})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr]\bigr|\lesssim 1.

(4.27)

Therefore, the claim follows if we prove that the term in the third line of (
4.26
) is of order one. More precisely, thanks to the definition of
b_{n}^{\prime}
, it is sufficient to prove that, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\upvartheta})\bigr]-\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\upvartheta_{+}})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr]\bigr|\lesssim 1.

Since, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough,
\smash{\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{a}}(\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-})}
is of constant order, this is equivalent to prove that

\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\upvartheta_{+}})\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr]\bigr|+\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\upvartheta_{-}})\mid\upvartheta_{-}<\upvartheta_{+}\bigr]\bigr|\lesssim 1,

where the implicit constant must be independent of everything else. We note that this inequality follows easily from Lemma
4.14
(see Figure
9
). Hence, putting everything together and going back to (
4.26
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{a}}\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}_{n}(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\upvartheta_{+}}))\mid\upvartheta_{+}<\upvartheta_{-}\bigr]+b_{n}^{\prime}\bigr|\lesssim\upeta_{n},

where the implicit constant is independent of everything else. Therefore, the desired claim follows from the previous inequality and from the fact that, thanks to Lemma
3.9
, it holds almost surely that

\bigl|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{1}))-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{2}))\bigr|\leq 2\upeta_{n},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}_{1},\smash{\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}}

Step 2:

In this step, we will actually prove the result in the lemma statement. To this end, we fix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{\overline{a}}^{\dagger,n}}
and we consider the dual edges
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{1}}
,
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\in\mathcal{E}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}_{{\overline{a}}}}
such that
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{1}^{+}=\widehat{\mathbf{x}}=\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{-}}
. Furthermore, we let
\smash{\mathbf{e}_{1}}
,
\smash{\mathbf{e}_{2}\in\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}_{{\overline{a}}}}
be the corresponding primal edges, and we let
\smash{\mathbf{x}_{1}}
,
\smash{\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}}
be the endpoints of
\mathbf{e}_{1}
and
\mathbf{e}_{2}
in the set
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}})^{-1}({\overline{a}})}
(see Figure
10
). At this point, we need to split the proof in two different cases:

•

If
\mathbf{x}_{1}\neq\mathbf{x}_{2}
, then, by construction of the Smith diagram, it holds almost surely that

\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})\in\bigl[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{n}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}(\mathbf{x}_{1})),\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{n}^{{\dagger},\rm{rand}}(\mathbf{x}_{2}))\bigr].

•

If
\mathbf{x}_{1}=\mathbf{x}_{2}
, then it holds that

\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})\in\bigl[\min\bigl\{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{v})\,:\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{x}_{1})\},\max\{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{v})\,:\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{x}_{1})\bigr\}\bigr],

where we recall that
\mathcal{S}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{x}_{1})
denotes the horizontal segment associated to
\mathbf{x}_{1}
by the lifted tiling map.

In both cases, if
\smash{\mathbf{x}_{1}}
,
\smash{\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}}
, then the conclusion follows from the previous step. However, in general, it could be that
\smash{\mathbf{x}_{1}\in\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}-(2\pi,0)}
or
\smash{\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}+(2\pi,0)}
. In both these cases, we cannot directly appeal to the previous step to conclude. Nevertheless, a simple application of Lemma
3.6
implies that the same result of the first step holds also for the vertices in
\smash{\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}-(2\pi,0)}
and in
\smash{\mathcal{P}^{{\dagger},n}_{\overline{a}}+(2\pi,0)}
. Therefore, this concludes the proof.
∎

Before proceeding with the proof of Lemma
4.10
, we need to state a lemma which is the dual counterpart of Lemma
4.14
.

Lemma 4.16
.

Fix
\upvarepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime}-1)
,
M\in\mathbb{N}
, and define the following event

\widehat{\mathrm{A}}_{M,\upvarepsilon}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}:=\bigl\{\exists\,s,t\in[0,\widehat{\uptau}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}]\,:\,\bigl|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}_{t})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}_{s})\bigr|>M;\;\bigl|\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}_{u})\bigr|\in[R^{\prime}-\upvarepsilon,R^{\prime}+\upvarepsilon],\;\forall u\in[s,t]\bigr\}.

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that, for any
n>n(R,\updelta,\upvarepsilon)
large enough, it holds that

\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathrm{A}}_{M,\upvarepsilon}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}\bigr)\lesssim\exp(-CM/\upvarepsilon),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall M\in\mathbb{N},

where the implicit constant is independent of everything else.

Proof.

The proof of this lemma can be done by employing a similar argument to that used in Lemma
4.14
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Lemma
4.10
, which is now an immediate consequence of the results proved above.

Proof of Lemma
4.10
.

The proof basically consists of putting together some of the previous results. More precisely, fix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}(R^{\prime}-1)}
and let
\smash{\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}_{\widehat{\uptau}_{\widehat{x}}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{\overline{a}}^{\dagger,n}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{\underline{a}}^{\dagger,n}}
be such that
\smash{\upsigma_{2\pi}(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}_{\widehat{\uptau}_{\widehat{x}}})=\upsigma_{2\pi}(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n,\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}_{\widehat{\uptau}_{\widehat{x}}})}
. Then, by Lemma
2.9
, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}\left[\frac{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\widehat{\uptau}_{\widehat{x}}})}{2\pi}-\frac{\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\widehat{\uptau}_{\widehat{x}}})+b^{\prime}_{n}}{\upeta_{n}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}\left[\frac{\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\widehat{\uptau}_{\widehat{x}}})}{2\pi}-\frac{\mathfrak{w}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\widehat{\uptau}_{\widehat{x}}})+b^{\prime}_{n}}{\upeta_{n}}\right].

Therefore, the result follows thanks to Lemma
4.15
if we show that also
\smash{\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}}[\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{n}_{\widehat{\uptau}_{\widehat{x}}})]\bigr|\lesssim 1}
. We note that this fact is easily implied by Lemma
4.16
, and so the proof is completed.
∎

4.3
Proof of Theorem
1.4

We are now ready to give a proof of the main theorem of this article. As we have already remarked, the proof of this theorem is a consequence of Propositions
4.3
and
4.8
.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Fix
R>1
,
\updelta\in(0,1)
, and consider a point
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(R)
. We divide the proof into three main steps.

Step 1.

By definition of the Smith embedding
\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}
, we have that
\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x}))=\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{x})
. Hence, from Proposition
4.3
, we know that there exist two real sequences
\{b^{\mathfrak{h}}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{c^{\mathfrak{h}}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, independent of
R
and
\updelta
, such that, for
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\bigl|c^{\mathfrak{h}}_{n}\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x}))+b^{\mathfrak{h}}_{n}-\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\bigr|\leq\frac{\updelta}{\sqrt{2}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(\mathbb{R}\times[-R,R]).

Step 2.

Let
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n,\downarrow}(\mathbf{x})=[\mathbf{e}_{k}]
be the set of harmonically oriented edges in
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}
with heads equal to
\mathbf{x}
ordered in such a way that
\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{1}\cdots\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{k}
forms a counter-clockwise oriented path in the lifted dual graph
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{{\dagger},n}
. Then, by construction of the Smith embedding
\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}
, we have that

\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x}))\in\bigl[\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{-}_{1}),\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{+}_{k})\bigr].

(4.28)

Therefore, letting
\{b^{\mathfrak{w}}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be the sequence in the statement of Proposition
4.8
, we have that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{2\pi}{\upeta_{n}}\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x}))+b^{\mathfrak{w}}_{n}-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\leq\left|\frac{2\pi}{\upeta_{n}}\mathfrak{w}_{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{1}^{-})+b^{\mathfrak{w}}_{n}-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{1}^{-})\right|+\bigl|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{-}_{1})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\bigr|+\frac{2\pi}{\upeta_{n}}\bigl|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{x}))-\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{1})\bigr|.

The first term on the right-hand side of the above expression is bounded by
\updelta/(5\sqrt{2})
thanks to Proposition
4.8
. The second term is also bounded by
\updelta/(5\sqrt{2})
since Lemma
4.2
rules out the existence of macroscopic faces. Concerning the third term, recalling (
4.28
), we have that

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{\upeta_{n}}\bigl|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{n}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{x}))-\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{1}^{-})\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\leq\left|\frac{2\pi}{\upeta_{n}}\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{1}^{-})+b^{\mathfrak{w}}_{n}-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{1}^{-})\right|+\left|\frac{2\pi}{\upeta_{n}}\mathfrak{w}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{+})+b^{\mathfrak{w}}_{n}-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{+})\right|+\bigl|\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{+})-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{1}^{-})\bigr|.

The first and second term on the right-hand side of the above expression are bounded by
\updelta/(5\sqrt{2})
, thanks to Proposition
4.8
. The third term is also bounded by
\updelta/(5\sqrt{2})
, for
n>n(R,\updelta)
thanks, once again, to Lemma
4.2
. We remark that all the previous bounds obviously hold only for
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough. Therefore, putting it all together, we obtain that, for all
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\left|\frac{2\pi}{\upeta_{n}}\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x}))+b^{\mathfrak{w}}_{n}-\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})\right|\leq\frac{\updelta}{\sqrt{2}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(\mathbb{R}\times[-R,R]).

Step 3.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the affine transformation
T^{{\dagger}}_{n}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
by letting

\mathop{Re}\nolimits(T^{{\dagger}}_{n}\mathbf{x}):=\frac{2\pi}{\upeta_{n}}\mathop{Re}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})+b_{n}^{\mathfrak{w}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{Im}\nolimits(T^{{\dagger}}_{n}\mathbf{x}):=c_{n}^{\mathfrak{h}}\mathop{Im}\nolimits(\mathbf{x})+b^{\mathfrak{h}}_{n},\hskip 20.00003pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],

Therefore, the previous two steps yield that, for any
n>n(R,\updelta)
large enough, it holds that

\mathrm{d}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bigl(T^{{\dagger}}_{n}\dot{\mathcal{S}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{x}\bigr)\leq\updelta,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{{\dagger},n}(\mathbb{R}\times[-R,R]),

where
\mathrm{d}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
denotes the Euclidean distance in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. This is obviously equivalent to the desired result, and so the proof is completed.
∎

5
Application to mated-CRT maps

The main goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1.5
. Roughly speaking, the plan is as follows. We will first introduce an a priori embedding of mated-CRT maps which is “close” to LQG. We then prove that this a priori embedding satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
1.4
. Finally, we show how this allows to conclude.

5.1
SLE/LQG description of mated-CRT maps

We now discuss an equivalent description of mated-CRT maps in terms of SLE/LQG, which comes from the results of
[
DMS21
]
. These results imply that mated-CRT maps can be realized as cell configurations constructed from space-filling
\mathrm{SLE}_{\kappa}
curves parameterized by quantum mass with respect to a certain independent LQG surface. We will not need many properties of the SLE/LQG objects involved, so we will not give detailed definitions, but we will give precise references instead.

Liouville quantum gravity surfaces.

For
\gamma\in(0,2)
and
D\subseteq\mathbb{C}
, a doubly marked
\gamma
-LQG surface is an
equivalence class of quadruples
(D,h,z_{1},z_{2})
where
h
is a random generalized function
on
D
(which we will always take to be an instance of some variant of the Gaussian free field),
and
z_{1}
,
z_{2}\in D
. Two such quadruples
\smash{(D,h,z_{1},z_{2})}
and
\smash{(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{h},\widetilde{z_{1}},\widetilde{z_{2}})}
are declared to be equivalent if there is a conformal map
f:\widetilde{D}\to D
such that

\widetilde{h}=h\circ f+Q\log|f^{\prime}|\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptf(\widetilde{z}_{1})=z_{1},\;f(\widetilde{z}_{2})=z_{2},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ where }\hskip 10.00002ptQ=\frac{2}{\gamma}+\frac{\gamma}{2}.

(5.1)

For
\gamma\in(0,2)
, it is well-known that one can construct a random measure, called the
\gamma
-LQG area measure, which is formally given by
\mu_{h}:=e^{\gamma h}\mathrm{d}^{2}z
, where
d^{2}z
denotes the Lebesgue measure on
D
. Since
h
is a random generalized function, this definition does not make rigorous sense and one should proceed using a standard regularization and limiting procedure
[
DS11
]
. The
\gamma
-LQG area measure satisfies a certain change of coordinates formula. More precisely, given two equivalent doubly marked
\gamma
-LQG surface
(D,h,z_{1},z_{2})
and
(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{h},\widetilde{z_{1}},\widetilde{z_{2}})
, then it holds almost surely that
\mu_{h}(f(A))=\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(A)
for all Borel sets
A\subseteq\widetilde{D}
, where
f:\widetilde{D}\to D
is a conformal map such that (
5.1
) holds.

In this article, we are interested in two different kind of doubly marked
\gamma
-LQG surfaces.

•

The
doubly marked quantum sphere

(\mathbb{C},h,0,\infty)
, where
h
is a variant of the Gaussian free field precisely defined in
[
DMS21
, Definition 4.21]
. For
\gamma\in(0,2)
, it is well-known that one can associate with the random generalized function
h
a random measure
\mu_{h}
on
\mathbb{C}
, the
\gamma
-LQG measure, with
\mu_{h}(\mathbb{C})<\infty
(again, we will not need the precise definition here). Typically, one considers a unit-area quantum sphere, which means that we fix
\mu_{h}(\mathbb{C})=1
.

•

The
0
-quantum cone

(\mathbb{C},h^{\rm{c}},0,\infty)
, where
h^{\rm{c}}
is a variant of the Gaussian free field precisely defined in
[
DMS21
, Definition 4.10]
. Also in this case, for
\gamma\in(0,2)
, we can associate to
h^{\rm{c}}
the
\gamma
-LQG measure
\mu_{h^{\rm c}}
which has infinite total mass, but it is locally finite.

Schramm–Loewner evolution.

We do not need to precisely define SLE
κ
, but rather it is sufficient to know that whole-plane space-filling SLE
κ
, for
\kappa>4
, is a random space-filling curve
\upvartheta
which travels from
\infty
to
\infty
in
\mathbb{C}
. It is a variant of SLE
κ

[
Sch00
]
which was introduced in
[
MS17
,
DMS21
]
. Space-filling SLE
κ
for
\kappa\geq 8
is a two-sided version of ordinary SLE
κ
(which is already space-filling), whereas space-filling SLE
κ
for
\kappa\in(4,8)
can be obtained from ordinary SLE
κ
by iteratively filling in the “bubbles” which the path disconnects from
\infty
.

Construction of the a priori embedding.

An important feature of the mated-CRT map is that it comes with an a priori embedding into
\mathbb{C}
described by SLE-decorated LQG. To explain this embedding, consider a doubly marked quantum sphere
(\mathbb{C},h,0,\infty)
and, for
\gamma\in(0,2)
, consider the associated
\gamma
-LQG measure
\mu_{h}
. Sample a space-filling SLE
κ
curve
\upvartheta
with
\kappa=16/\gamma^{2}
, independently from the random generalized function
h
, and reparametrize
\upvartheta
so that

\upvartheta(0)=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\mu_{h}\bigl(\upvartheta([a,b]\bigr)=b-a,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall a,b\in\mathbb{R}\text{ with }a<b.

For
\gamma\in(0,2)
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the
n
-
structure graph

\mathcal{G}^{n}
associated with the pair
(h,\upvartheta)
as follows. The vertex set of
\mathcal{G}^{n}
is given by

\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{Z}\cap(0,1].

Two distinct vertices
x_{1}
,
x_{2}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}
are connected by one edge (resp. two edges) if and only if the intersection of the corresponding cells
\upvartheta([x_{1}-1/n,x_{1}])
and
\upvartheta([x_{2}-1/n,x_{2}])
has one connected component which is not a singleton (resp. two connected components which are not singletons). We refer to Figure
11
for a diagrammatic construction of the SLE/LQG embedding of the mated-CRT map.

Figure 11:
Left:
A space-filling SLE
κ
curve
\upvartheta
, for
\kappa\geq 8
, divided into cells
\upvartheta([x-1/n,x])
for a collection of
x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}
. Each cell has
\mu_{h}
-measure equal to
1/n
.
Middle:
The same as in the left figure but with a orange path showing the order in which cells are traversed by
\upvartheta
.
Right:
In each cell we drawn a red point corresponding to a vertex in
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}
. Two vertices are connected by a red edge if the corresponding cells intersect. This illustrates how the SLE/LQG embedding of the
n
-mated CRT map with the sphere topology is built. A similar figure has appeared in
[
GMS19
]
.

When
\kappa\geq 8
, the intersection of cells
\upvartheta([x_{1}-1/n,x_{1}])\cap\upvartheta([x_{2}-1/n,x_{2}])
is always either empty or the union of one or two non-singleton connected components. However, when
\kappa\in(4,8)
it is possible that
\upvartheta([x_{1}-1/n,x_{1}])\cap\upvartheta([x_{2}-1/n,x_{2}])
is a totally disconnected Cantor-like set, in which case
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are not joint by an edge in
\mathcal{G}^{n}
(see Figure
12
).

Figure 12:
A typical space-filling SLE
κ
curve
\upvartheta
, for
\kappa\in(4,8)
, divided into cells. The picture is slightly misleading since the set of “pinch point” where the left and right boundaries of each cell meet is actually uncountable. The black dots indicate the points where
\upvartheta
starts and finishes filling each cell. Note that the gray and green cells intersect at several points, but do not share a connected boundary arc so are not considered to be adjacent. A similar figure has appeared in
[
GMS21
]
.

The following result explains the connection between the pair
(h,\upvartheta)
and the mated-CRT map
\mathcal{G}^{n}
, and it is a consequence of
[
DMS21
, Theorems 1.9 and 8.18]
.

Proposition 5.1
.

The family of structure graphs
\{\mathcal{G}^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
agrees in law with the family of
n
-mated-CRT maps with the sphere topology defined in Subsection
1.3
.

The previous proposition gives us an a priori embedding of the mated-CRT map in
\mathbb{C}
by sending each
x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}
to the point
\upvartheta(x)\in\mathbb{C}
. Furthermore, the graph
\mathcal{G}^{n}
comes naturally with two marked vertices. Indeed, we can let
v^{n}_{0}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}
(resp.
v^{n}_{1}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}
) to be the vertex corresponding to the cell containing
0
(resp.
\infty
). We also emphasize that here all the edges in
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{G}^{n}
are assumed to have unit conductance.

Construction of the a priori embedding of the dual graph.

The a priori SLE/LQG embedding of
\smash{\mathcal{G}^{n}}
also induces an a priori embedding into
\mathbb{C}
of the associated planar dual graph
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{n}}
. Indeed, each vertex of
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{n}}
is naturally identified with the set of points in
\mathbb{C}
where three of the cells
\upvartheta([x-1/n,x])
meet
6
6

6

Note that there cannot be more than three cells meeting at a single point or we would have a face of degree greater than three.
, for
x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}
. The set of edges of
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{n}}
can be identified with the boundary segments of the cells which connect these vertices. To be precise, this is not an embedding when
\kappa\in(4,8)
since the edges can intersect (but not cross) each other (see Figure
12
). To deal with this case, we can very slightly perturb the edges so that they do not intersect except at their endpoints. We refer to the proof of Proposition
5.4
for more details on how one can handle this situation.

5.2
Mated-CRT maps satisfy the assumptions

In this subsection, we show that the a priori embedding of mated-CRT maps with the sphere topology satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
1.4
. In particular, we will show here that assumptions
(H2)
and
(H3)
are satisfied in this specific case. We recall that, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\gamma\in(0,2)
, the mated-CRT map
\mathcal{G}^{n}
comes with two marked vertices
v_{0}^{n}
and
v_{1}^{n}
which correspond to the cell containing
0
and
\infty
, respectively

Proposition 5.2

(Invariance principle on the mated-CRT map,
[
GMS21
, Theorem 3.4]
)
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\gamma\in(0,2)
, let
\mathcal{G}^{n}
be the
n
-mated CRT map with the sphere topology embedded in
\mathbb{C}
under the a priori SLE/LQG embedding specified in Subsection
5.1
. View the embedded random walk on
\mathcal{G}^{n}
, stopped when it hits
v_{1}^{n}
, as a continuous curve in
\mathbb{C}
obtained by piecewise linear interpolation at constant speed. For each compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{C}
and for any
z\in K
, the conditional law given the pair
(h,\upvartheta)
of the random walk on
\mathcal{G}^{n}
started from the vertex
x_{z}^{n}\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{G}^{n}
nearest to
z
weakly converges in probability as
n\to\infty
to the law of the Brownian motion on
\mathbb{C}
started from
z
with respect to the local topology on curves viewed modulo time parameterization specified in Subsection
2.1.2
, uniformly over all
z\in K
.

Remark 5.3
.

To be precise, in
[
GMS21
, Theorem 3.4]
, the above theorem is stated for a mated-CRT map built using a quantum sphere with only one marked point. However, the quantum sphere with two marked points can be obtained from the quantum sphere with one marked point
(\mathbb{C},h,\infty)
by first sampling a point
z\in\mathbb{C}
uniformly from the
\gamma
-LQG area measure
\mu_{h}
, then applying a conformal map which sends
z
to
0
(see
[
DMS21
, Proposition A.13]
). Therefore, since Brownian motion is conformally invariant, the statement in
[
GMS21
, Theorem 3.4]
immediately implies Proposition
5.2
for the quantum sphere with two marked points.

The main purpose of this subsection is to prove that an analogous result holds also on the sequence of a priori SLE/LQG embedding of the dual
n
-mated-CRT map with the sphere topology. More precisely, we want to prove the following proposition.

Proposition 5.4

(Invariance principle on the dual mated-CRT map)
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\gamma\in(0,2)
, let
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{n}
be the dual planar graph associated to the
n
-mated-CRT map
\mathcal{G}^{n}
with the sphere topology embedded in
\mathbb{C}
under the a priori embedding specified in Subsection
5.1
. View the embedded random walk on
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{n}
as a continuous curve in
\mathbb{C}
obtained by piecewise linear interpolation at constant speed. For each compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{C}
and for any
z\in K
, the conditional law given the pair
(h,\upvartheta)
of the random walk on
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{n}
started from the vertex
\widehat{x}_{z}^{n}\in\mathcal{V}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{n}
nearest to
z
weakly converges as
n\to\infty
to the law of the Brownian motion on
\mathbb{C}
started from
z
with respect to the local topology on curves viewed modulo time parameterization specified in Subsection
2.1.2
, uniformly over all
z\in K
.

5.2.1
Invariance principle on embedded lattices

In order to prove Proposition
5.4
, we need to take a step back and state a general theorem from
[
GMS22
]
which guarantees that the random walk on certain embedded lattices has Brownian motion as a scaling limit. For the reader’s convenience, we recall here the main definitions needed and in what follows we adopt notation similar to that of
[
GMS22
]
.

Definition 5.5
.

An embedded lattice
\mathcal{M}
is a graph embedded in
\mathbb{C}
in such a way that each edge is a simple curve with zero Lebesgue measure, the edges intersect only at their endpoints, and each compact subset of
\mathbb{C}
intersects at most finitely many edges of
\mathcal{M}
. As usual, we write
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{M}
for the set of vertices of
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{M}
for the set of edges of
\mathcal{M}
, and
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{M}
for the set of faces of
\mathcal{M}
.

Definition 5.6
.

For an embedded lattice
\mathcal{M}
and
x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{M}
, we define the
outradius
of
x
by setting

\Out(x):=\diam\left(\bigcup_{\{H\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{M}\,:\,x\in\partial H\}}H\right),

i.e., the diameter of the union of the faces with
x
on their boundaries. Here
\partial H
denotes the boundary of the face
H\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{M}
.

For
C>0
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we write
C(\mathcal{M}-z)
for the embedded lattice obtained by first translating everything by the amount
-z
and then by scaling everything by the factor
C
. We are interested in random embedded lattices that satisfy the following assumptions.

(I1)

(
Translation invariance modulo scaling
) There is a (possibly random and
\mathcal{M}
-dependent) increasing sequence of open sets
U_{j}\subset\mathbb{C}
, each of which is either a square or a disk, whose union is all of
\mathbb{C}
such that the following is true. Conditional on
\mathcal{M}
and
U_{j}
, let
z_{j}
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
be sampled uniformly from the Lebesgue measure on
U_{j}
. Then the shifted lattice
\mathcal{M}-z_{j}
converge in law to
\mathcal{M}
modulo scaling as
j\to\infty
, i.e., there are random numbers
C_{j}>0
(possibly depending on
\mathcal{M}
and
z_{j}
) such that
C_{j}(\mathcal{M}-z_{j})\to\mathcal{M}
in law with respect to the metric specified in
[
GMS22
, Equation (1.6)]
.
7
7

7

Several equivalent formulations of this condition are given in
[
GMS22
, Definition 1.2]
.

(I2)

(
Ergodicity modulo scaling
) Every real-valued measurable function
F=F(\mathcal{M})
which is invariant under translation and scaling, i.e.,
F(C(\mathcal{M}-z))=F(\mathcal{M})
for each
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
C>0
, is almost surely equal to a deterministic constant.

(I3)

(
Finite expectation
) Let
H_{0}\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{M}
be the face of
\mathcal{M}
containing 0, then

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{M}\cap\partial H_{0}}\frac{\Out(x)^{2}\mathop{deg}\nolimits(x)}{\Area(H_{0})}\right]<\infty,

where, in this case,
\mathop{deg}\nolimits(x)
denotes the number of edges incident to
x
.

Proposition 5.7

(
[
GMS22
, Theorem 1.11]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an embedded lattice satisfying assumptions
(I1)
,
(I2)
,
(I3)
. For
\upvarepsilon>0
, view the embedded random walk on
\upvarepsilon\mathcal{M}
as a continuous curve in
\mathbb{C}
obtained by piecewise linear interpolation at constant speed. For each compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{C}
and for any
z\in K
, the conditional law given
\mathcal{M}
of the random walk on
\upvarepsilon\mathcal{M}
started from the vertex
\widehat{x}_{z}^{\upvarepsilon}\in\mathcal{V}(\upvarepsilon\mathcal{M})
nearest to
z
weakly converges in probability as
\upvarepsilon\to 0
to the law of a Brownian motion on
\mathbb{C}
, with some deterministic, non-degenerate covariance matrix
\Sigma
, started from
z
with respect to the local topology on curves viewed modulo time parameterization specified in Subsection
2.1.2
, uniformly over all
z\in K
.

To be precise, the above theorem follows from the proof
[
GMS22
, Theorem 1.11]
(which gives the same statement but without the uniform rate of convergence on compact subsets) by using
[
GMS22
, Theorem 3.10]
in place of
[
GMS22
, Theorem 1.16]
.

5.2.2
Proof of Proposition
5.4

The purpose of this subsection is to transfer the general result of Proposition
5.7
to the particular setting of Proposition
5.4
. In order to do this, we need to proceed in two steps. First, we verify that the hypothesis of Proposition
5.7
are satisfied for a sequence of embedded lattices built through the
0
-quantum cone. Then, we transfer the result to the sequence of
n
-mated CRT maps with the sphere topology by means of an absolute continuity argument.

Let
(\mathbb{C},h^{\rm{c}},0,\infty)
be a 0-quantum cone as specified in Subsection
5.1
. We now define a graph associated with the 0-quantum cone in a way which is exactly analogous to the SLE/LQG description of mated-CRT maps with the sphere topology described in Section
5.1
.
For
\gamma\in(0,2)
, let
\upvartheta
be a whole-plane space-filling SLE
κ
, with
\kappa=16/\gamma^{2}
, sampled independently from
h^{\rm{c}}
and then parameterized by the
\gamma
-LQG measure
\mu_{h^{\rm{c}}}
in such a way that
\upvartheta(0)=0
. Let
\upxi
be sampled uniformly from the unit interval
[0,1]
, independently from everything else, and let

\mathcal{H}:=\bigl\{\upvartheta\bigl([x-1,x]\bigr)\,:\,x\in\mathbb{Z}+\upxi\bigr\}.

The reason for considering times in
\mathbb{Z}+\upxi
instead just in
\mathbb{Z}
is to avoid making the point
0=\upvartheta(0)
special. This is needed in order to check the translation invariance modulo scaling hypothesis
(I1)
.
We view
\mathcal{H}
as a planar map whose vertex set is
\mathcal{H}
itself. Two vertices
H
,
H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
are joined by one edge (resp. two edges) if and only if
H
and
H^{\prime}
intersect along one (resp. two) non-trivial connected boundary arc. (resp. arcs). For
H
,
H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
, with
H\neq H^{\prime}
, we write
H\sim H^{\prime}
if they are joined by at least one edge. Given a set
A\subset\mathbb{C}
, we write

\mathcal{H}(A):=\bigl\{H\in\mathcal{H}\,:\,H\cap A\neq\emptyset\bigr\}

and moreover, for
H\in\mathcal{H}
, we write
\mathop{deg}\nolimits(H)
for the number of cells
H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
(counted with edge multiplicity) such that
H\sim H^{\prime}
.

Proof of Proposition
5.4
.

For
\gamma\in(0,2)
, define the cell configuration
\mathcal{H}
associated with a space-filling SLE
κ
on a
0
-quantum cone as specified above. Let
\mathcal{M}
be the embedded defined as follows.

•

The vertex set of
\mathcal{M}
consists of points
x\in\mathbb{C}
such that there are three cells in
\mathcal{H}
that meet at
x
and with the property that the boundary of each of such cells has a connected subset that touches
x
;

•

The edge set of
\mathcal{M}
consists of the boundary segments of the cells which connect the vertices.

In other words,
\mathcal{M}
is nothing but the planar dual of
\mathcal{H}
. Moreover, by construction,
\mathcal{M}
is an embedded lattice in the sense of Definition
5.5
, except that embedded edges are allowed to intersect but not cross, in the case when
\gamma\in(\sqrt{2},2)
. To deal with this case, we can consider a different embedding of
\mathcal{M}
in which all the vertices occupy the same position, but the edges are slightly perturbed so that they do not intersect except at their endpoints. More precisely, for each edge with touching points in its interior, we can slightly perturb it in such a way that the modification only depends on the position of the edge itself and on the positions of the adjacent faces. In particular, this perturbation can be carried out in a translation and scaling invariant way. Therefore, since this procedure only depends on the local configuration of the lattice, if the starting cell configuration is translation invariant modulo scaling, then also the perturbed lattice is translation invariant modulo scaling.

We will now check that
\mathcal{M}
satisfies assumptions
(I1)
,
(I2)
, and
(I3)
. The translation invariance modulo scaling assumption
(I1)
and the ergodicity modulo scaling assumption
(I2)
follows from the corresponding properties for the associated cell configuration
\mathcal{H}
as checked in
[
GMS21
, Proposition 3.1]
. Therefore, we can just focus on proving the finite expectation assumption
(I3)
. To this end, recalling that the cell
H_{0}
is the face of
\mathcal{M}
containing
0
, we proceed as follows

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{M}\cap\partial H_{0}}\frac{\Out(x)^{2}\mathop{deg}\nolimits(x)}{\Area(H_{0})}

\displaystyle\leq 12\sum_{x\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{M}\cap\partial H_{0}}\sum_{\{H\in\mathcal{H}\,:\,x\in\partial H\}}\frac{\diam(H)^{2}}{\Area(H_{0})}

(5.2)

\displaystyle\leq 48\sum_{\{H\in\mathcal{H}\,:\,H\sim H_{0}\}}\frac{\diam(H)^{2}}{\Area(H_{0})}+12\frac{\diam(H_{0})^{2}}{\Area(H_{0})}\mathop{deg}\nolimits(H_{0}).

In the first line of (
5.2
), we used the fact that each vertex of
\mathcal{M}
has degree at most
3
and the inequality
(a+b+c)^{2}\leq 4(a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2})
. In the second line, we use that each cell
H\in\mathcal{H}
with
H\sim H_{0}
intersects
H_{0}
along at most two disjoint connected boundary arcs (one on its left boundary and one on its right boundary), so there are at most
4
vertices of
\mathcal{M}
in
H\cap H_{0}
. Now, we notice that the second quantity in the last line of (
5.2
) has finite expectation thanks to
[
GMS21
, Theorem 4.1]
. Therefore, we can just focus our attention to the sum appearing in the last line of (
5.2
). Basically, the fact that this sum has finite expectation follows from the combination of several results in
[
GMS21
]
and
[
GMS22
]
.

Let
F=F(\mathcal{H})
denote the sum appearing in the last line of (
5.2
). We will show that
\mathbb{E}[F]<\infty
using an ergodic theory result from
[
GMS22
, Section 2.2]
. Let
\{S_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the bi-infinite sequence of origin-containing squares of a uniform dyadic system independent from
\mathcal{H}
, as defined in
[
GMS22
, Section 2.1]
. We will not need the precise definition of these sequence here, but rather we only need to know
S_{k-1}\subset S_{k}
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\cup_{k=1}^{\infty}S_{k}=\mathbb{C}
. As shown in
[
GMS21
, Proposition 3.1]
, the cell configuration
\mathcal{H}
satisfies a suitable translation invariance modulo scaling assumption, and so, we can apply
[
GMS22
, Lemma 2.7]
to
\mathcal{H}
to find that the following is true. If we let
F_{z}
for
z\in\mathbb{C}
be defined in the same manner as
F
but with the translated cell configuration
\mathcal{H}-z
in place of
\mathcal{H}
, then it holds almost surely that

\mathbb{E}[F]=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\Area(S_{k})}\int_{S_{k}}F_{z}dz.

To bound the right-hand side of the above expression, we can proceed as follows

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\Area(S_{k})}\int_{S_{k}}F_{z}dz

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\Area(S_{k})}\int_{S_{k}}\sum_{\{H\in\mathcal{H}\,:\,H\sim H_{z}\}}\frac{\diam(H)^{2}}{\Area(H_{z})}dz

(5.3)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\Area(S_{k})}\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}(S_{k})}\sum_{\{H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}\,:\,H^{\prime}\sim H\}}\diam(H^{\prime})^{2}.

Since the maximal size of the cells in
\mathcal{H}(S_{k})
is almost surely of strictly smaller order than the side length of
S_{k}
as
k\to\infty
(see
[
GMS22
, Lemma 2.9]
), we find that almost surely for large enough
k\in\mathbb{N}
, each
H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
with
H^{\prime}\sim H
for some
H\in\mathcal{H}(S_{k})
is contained in
\mathcal{H}(S_{k}(1))
, where
S_{k}(1)
is the square with the same center as
S_{k}
and three times the side length of
S_{k}
. Therefore, the last line in (
5.3
) is almost surely bounded above by

2\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\Area(S_{k})}\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}(S_{k}(1))}\diam(H)^{2}\mathop{deg}\nolimits(H),

which is finite by
[
GMS22
, Lemmas 2.8 and 2.10]
.

Therefore, summing up, we proved that the embedded lattice
\mathcal{M}
satisfies assumptions
(I1)
,
(I2)
,
(I3)
, ad so, we can apply Proposition
5.7
. Furthermore, we notice that, thanks to the rotational invariance of the law of
(h^{\rm{c}},\upvartheta)
, the limiting covariance matrix
\Sigma
is given by a positive scalar multiple of the identity matrix. Hence, in order to conclude the proof of Proposition
5.4
, we need to transfer the result to the setting of mated-CRT maps with the sphere topology. This can be done by means of absolute continuity arguments as in
[
GMS21
, Subsection 3.3]
. For the sake of brevity, we will not repeat such arguments here and we refer to
[
GMS21
]
.
∎

5.3
Convergence to LQG

In this subsection, we see how the proof of Theorem
1.5
is almost an immediate consequence of the results proved above. More specifically, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\gamma\in(0,2)
, let
(\mathcal{G}^{n},v_{0}^{n},v_{1}^{n})
be the doubly marked
n
-mated-CRT map with the sphere topology under the a priori SLE/LQG embedding as specified in Subsection
5.1
.
We observe that there exists a conformal map from
\mathbb{C}
to
\mathcal{C}_{2\pi}
sending
\infty\mapsto+\infty
and
0\mapsto-\infty
. This mapping is unique up to horizontal translation and rotation. The horizontal translation can be fixed by specifying that the volume of
\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}\times[0,\infty)
under the
\gamma
-LQG measure
\mu_{h}
